Near solves

A target where a solver beat its matched control by a reproducible margin, or a control showed a negative was not a real test, stays here until its named next step has run or a verifier has classed it in AUDIT.md (CLAUDE.md rule 5). A row untouched for 48 hours is marked stale, a flag for the parent, not a reason to drop it.

Readings, by how far the verifier could take them

One row per reading a separate verifier has classed. N4 means the principal editions, catalogues and project pages were searched and no prior decipherment was located; it is not a claim that the letter was never read. Open a row for the passage, the rating, who to tell and the text to send.

Whose key: our key we recovered it ourselves; period key, rebuilt by us from a decipherment, key sheet or cipher book of the time; published key someone else's, which we applied. A reading with our key at N3 or better is the nearest honest thing to a first: no earlier decipherment was found, and the key is our own work.

  • What it is

    The first solver reading to pass a novelty audit: tools/homophonic_anneal.py read the enclosure with a synthetic control of the same design at 99.3 percent; a verifier, an adversarial second audit and an N4 decision (10:56 UTC) found no prior decipherment or printed plaintext. Its sibling 57 and letter 126 were read by alignment and are N4 as well.

    N4 (no prior decipherment located; AUDIT.md 'N4 decision (D2)', two audits); S 238, M 126 of 364 tokens; presented as a PROPOSED reading (a third of the signs at grade M; the WVO editor's request, 26 Sept 2026); the KHA Collectie Japikse 20th-century copy is an unseen witness that the editor names as the likeliest place for an earlier decipherment -- N-class referred to the verifier lane (ROOM 26 Sept)

    What the passage says, and why it matters

    confirms or adds detail -- the Spanish and Navarre rumours are printed from other senders; the cipher adds Orange's own judgement (cryptanalytic reading, 35% M)

    Letter. William of Orange to Elector August, Breda, 24 Oct 1561. Autograph postscript, partly in cipher. Dresden
    HStA Loc. 9941/3 f.266r-v, WVO 53. No edition (Japikse's edition ends in September 1561). WVO summary of the clear
    letter: safe arrival at Breda, news of Spain and France, dogs and ferrets sent.

    Reading (`reading_53.txt`; 364 tokens: S 238, M 126. Cryptanalytic: key recovered without a key source,
    matched control 99.3%):
    > "neiuuer zeittung hab ich itc malsnicht zuberzuschreiben dan das dem printzen zu hispanien seines hern vatters
    > schuuester ehlich uermahlet uuerden und hieruber diesse lande zu uuregieren khomen sollen und dan ein gemein
    > geschret ist ... uuolle der hertzog uon uandosmen sein konigreich nauarra mit der gute oder krieg uuiederholen
    > mrch dunck aber die uueil der konnig zu franckreich noch so iungist es uuerde keinen furgang geuuinnen"

    *Of new tidings I have nothing to write except that the Prince of Spain is to marry his father's sister and come to
    govern these lands. There is also a common rumour that the Duke of Vendôme will recover his kingdom of Navarre by
    agreement or by war. But I think that, the King of France being still so young, it will come to nothing.*

    Already known. Both rumours are in print from other senders: the 1561 talk of a match between Don Carlos and his
    aunt Juana, and Antoine de Bourbon's Navarre claim.

    What the cipher adds. Orange's own judgement on the Navarre rumour, and the fact that he passed the Spanish
    rumours to August in cipher.

    Moment. Orange married Anna of Saxony, August's niece, at Leipzig in August 1561 *(context for the date)*. The
    couple reached Breda on 4 October (Demandt's regest of letter 57). This postscript comes three weeks later, as the new
    in-law alliance begins its correspondence. Rumours that Don Carlos would come to govern the Netherlands matter here,
    because Orange sat on the Council of State.

    Significance: confirms or adds detail to what the edition gives. The news is known. What it adds is Orange's
    opinion. It is a cryptanalytic reading with 35% M, and the second page (about 90 signs) is not fully read.

    Who to tell

    resources@huygens.knaw.nl, cc info@huygens.knaw.nl Huygens: sent, awaiting reply

    One note covers 2 readings (lodewijk-van-nassau-1573-74, august-van-saksen-1561-64); send it once and tick it once.

    sent 24 Sept 2026 (by the person); reply received 26 Sept 2026 from dr. E.C.M. (Ineke) Huysman, senior onderzoeker NL-Lab, Huygens Instituut -- see 'Reply' below; answer drafted in outreach/huygens-reply-2026-09-26.md for the person to send

    To
    resources@huygens.knaw.nl, cc info@huygens.knaw.nl
    Subject
    Cipher passages in six WVO letters (4610, 4611, 4616; 53, 57, 126) read with contemporary keys
    Body
    Dear editors of the Correspondence of William of Orange,

I run a small project that reads cipher passages in published letter images, with each reading checked by a separate search for any prior decipherment. Six letters in your William of Orange database carry cipher that the database records as unread. We have read them in part, with keys taken from contemporary decipherments of sibling letters in the same database, and located no prior decipherment in the principal editions after a logged search. None changes the story the editions tell; each adds a detail or shows the secret channel.

- WVO 4610, 4611, 4616, Lodewijk van Nassau to Orange, 3 June and 2 July 1573 and 12 April 1574 (two days before Mookerheyde). Key from the decipherments of 4613 and 4615. The cipher gives Louis's side of exchanges whose replies Groen prints (Archives, 1st ser., IV): a proposal to convene the Electors and French contacts; recruiting, with an offer of twenty companies for Friesland; and, in 1574, march and lodging.
- WVO 53, Orange to Elector August, 24 Oct 1561. Read without any key source (a cryptanalytic result, 238 of 364 signs at grade S): news of the Prince of Spain's marriage and the Netherlands, Vendôme and Navarre, and Orange's own judgement that it would come to nothing. The rumours are printed from other senders.
- WVO 57, August to Orange, 18 Nov 1561, read with the key of Orange's 1562 cipher: the Emperor's approach about electing Maximilian King of the Romans, told to Orange in secret within days of the Palatine letters Kluckhohn prints.
- WVO 126, Orange to August, 16 Sept 1564, read with a key from WVO 98: the Queen of Spain's miscarriage, printed from Saint-Sulpice; the cipher adds only the channel.

Readings, keys, per-token grades and the full search log for each: https://github.com/noautopilot/cipher-lab/tree/main/ciphers/lodewijk-van-nassau-1573-74 and https://github.com/noautopilot/cipher-lab/tree/main/ciphers/august-van-saksen-1561-64 (AUDIT.md in each). Images are your own record pages (brief?nr=4610, 4611, 4616, 53, 57, 126). If your editors know a decipherment of any of these, in the KHA or Dresden minutes or elsewhere, we would be glad to cite it.

If useful, we can hand over a table of the 73 WVO letters we found with cipher passages, marked which an edition prints in clear and which stand unread, and the keys as machine-readable tables, in whatever format suits the database. And if there are unread passages you would most like read, name them and we will try those first.

## Reply (received 26 Sept 2026, dr. Ineke Huysman, Huygens Instituut; pasted by the person; recorded as data)

Observations, each with what it changes here:

1. **WVO 53 and 126: the KHA holds twentieth-century copies in the Collectie Japikse.** Japikse printed cipher passages deciphered in his first volume (1934); these copies were preparation for a continuation that never appeared. She judges them the most likely place for an earlier decipherment, more so than the editions. -> Both audits list the Japikse copy as an unseen witness (AUDIT.md V3 and A2). An editor of the database now names it the first place to look, so the "no prior decipherment located" sentence for 53 and 126 must carry that witness by name until it is seen; the N-class question is referred to the verifier lane (ROOM, 26 Sept). Access needs a person at the KHA: ASKS row 67. 2. **126 and 57: the minutes (KHA A11-XIVi-04-26 "met een 'Zeitung'"; Dresden Loc. 9941/3 f.268-269 "met een 'Zettel'") may simply carry the text in clear.** -> Already in AUDIT.md (2.3, A2.2 item 2); keep the sentence in every summary of the results. 3. **The Palatine audience in Kluckhohn (nr. 148) dates from 30-31 December 1561, six weeks after August's letter of 18 November, not within days.** -> The audit had the date right (AUDIT.md D2.1 row 1, V3c row 9); the sent email and N4-READINGS.md said "within days" -- both corrected 26 Sept 2026. 4. **53 rests entirely on a recovered key, with a third of the signs uncertain: present it as a proposal, not a text.** -> Adopted: the status.json row, CONTRIBUTIONS.md and every summary say "proposed reading" for 53. 5. Two questions before she takes it further: (a) who is responsible for the project and how the work is divided between people and AI agents -- "it should be stated plainly in any contact with editors"; (b) what we intend to do with the results (publication, deposit with the holding archives, or only the repository). -> The person answers both; the disclosure sentence is now a standing rule for every outreach draft (outreach/README.md).

    Links a recipient can check

    folder audit reading image

  • What it is

    Key of 395 groups (grade C) from the contemporary decipherments of BLA 179, 185, 187, 188, 189, 190 and 194; one code for 1725-29 by leave-one-item-out (0.68-0.91). Partial readings: BLA 186 (Abbé Pareti to Marchmont, Madrid, 13 Sept 1728, Ripperda's escape: 20 of 24 cipher groups; the clear part of this letter was printed in English by Rose, Marchmont Papers 1831 ii 414-15, with the cipher shown only as '(Cypher.)'), BLA 191 enclosure (a) (106 of 141) and 3 of 7 code words in BLA 184. No prior decipherment located in the Huntington collection, HMC and Marchmont prints on IA, TNA Discovery descriptions or Google Books (24 Sept 2026). C 129, M 22, U 21 of 172.

    N4 each (no prior decipherment located; cipher passages only; partial; AUDIT.md 'N4 set', 24 Sept 2026); BLA 186's clear text printed in Rose 1831 ii 414-15

    What the passage says, and why it matters

    confirms or adds detail -- an agent's request for orders; BLA 192's clear text already carries the complaint about Keene

    Letter. Undated cipher enclosure from Port Ste Marie, forwarded from Cessnock to the Duke of Newcastle on 8 Aug
    1729. Huntington mssBLA 191 (pointer 61008, p5). The writer is not named. Pareti is probable for the run but not
    established for this item. No print. The copy sent to Newcastle has not been traced.

    Reading (`reading.txt`; 141 tokens: C 106, M 19, U 16). Rendering from NOTES.md, where the joins are interpretation:
    > "L'[805] que je vous ai, milord, est bien grande pour votre lettre du [6] ... comme je [285]is agit apres le depart du
    > [214], car Monsieur Keene m'a[i]me, mais il n'a aucun ordre des [1019][711]s pour moi, quoyque dans l'affaire passee je
    > lui ai fait [1118] bon [1052]ce, sans qu'il m'ait jamais dit ce que je pouvois fournir pour le mieux a l'accommodement
    > ... et je serai inutile dans l'[585=ig]norance. Mandez-moi donc quelque ordre pour ma [222]."

    *He thanks "milord" for his letter. Keene (the British minister) likes him but has no orders about him. He gave Keene
    good service in "the past affair" but was never told what he could contribute to the accommodation. He will be useless
    without instructions, so please send orders.* "milord", "serai" and "ignorance" rest on M or I values.

    Already known. BLA 192 (Port Ste Marie, 11 Aug 1729) is a clear letter on the same theme, with bracketed passages
    that went into cipher ("je n'ai pu trouver aucun secours dans Monsieur Kenne"). It is a different text.

    What the cipher adds. An agent's complaint about his standing with Keene during the "accommodement".

    Moment. *(context)* In summer 1729 Britain and Spain were negotiating the settlement signed as the Treaty of
    Seville (November 1729). The court was at Seville and Port Ste Marie (El Puerto de Santa María).

    Significance: confirms or adds detail to what the edition gives. There is no edition. BLA 192's clear text already
    carries the complaint, so the cipher adds the agent's own framing and no diplomatic news.

    Who to tell

    reference@huntington.org Huntington: sent, awaiting reply

    One note covers 2 readings (eckert-1864, huntington-blathwayt-madrid-1728); send it once and tick it once.

    sent 24 Sept 2026 (by the person; reply pending)

    To
    reference@huntington.org
    Subject
    Two Civil War telegrams in mssEC 19 and two Blathwayt cipher passages (mssBLA 186, 191) read from your collections
    Body
    Dear Reference Services,

I run a small project that reads cipher passages in digitised letters, with each reading checked by a separate search for any prior decipherment. Two groups of items in your collections are involved, and I would like to hand you the results and ask whether they are of use.

- Eckert papers, mssEC 41 (the 1864 cipher book) and mssEC 19: two telegrams to General Butler of 21 and 22 April 1864, Fox to Butler and Meigs to Butler, read with the book's key. Their substance is in print from the clear copies (Official Records and Butler's correspondence); what we add is the cipher form, the key as a machine-readable table, and twenty 1864 ledger entries read with it. No prior decipherment of the two telegrams was located in the Official Records, Butler's and Lincoln's editions, the Decoding the Civil War project pages or the catalogues.
- Blathwayt papers addenda, mssBLA 186 and 191(a), Madrid and Port St Mary intelligence of 1728-29, read with a key rebuilt from seven items in the same run that carry contemporary decipherments (395 groups). The clear part of BLA 186 is printed by Rose (Marchmont Papers, 1831, ii 414-15) with the cipher shown only as "(Cypher.)"; the cipher gives an agent's request for orders and Pareti's report on Ripperda's escape. No prior decipherment located in the Huntington and HMC catalogues, Rose, or full-text search.

Every reading is regenerated by a script from our transcription and the key, and every token is graded (H from a key source, C from known plaintext, M uncertain). Folders with the keys, readings and the full search log: https://github.com/noautopilot/cipher-lab/tree/main/ciphers/eckert-1864 and https://github.com/noautopilot/cipher-lab/tree/main/ciphers/huntington-blathwayt-madrid-1728 (AUDIT.md in each). If the Library or the project already holds a decipherment of any of these, we would be glad to know and will correct our notes.

  • What it is

    Read with the key Tomokiyo published: 509 of 638 cipher tokens at grade H, about 23 of 37 lines continuous French, 70 signs unkeyed; no contemporary decipherment. No prior decipherment or printed plaintext located in the Danzay editions, the Scottish and English state-paper series or the Danish regesta (AUDIT.md, two audits, 24 Sept 2026). Partial. The letter ends on f.36r (Copenhagen, signed Dantzay), same class N3 (AUDIT.md 'f.36r'): 37 of 67 cipher tokens at grade H, 'avecques le peu de perte pour le change' and 'par deça' read; three stretches and the f.35v/f.36r join unread. A blind second reader agreed on 86.8 percent of signs; 11 of its 12 proposed changes were accepted on the image and applied.

    N4 (no prior decipherment located)

    What the passage says, and why it matters

    confirms or adds detail -- adds the chancellor's role and an Augsburg-Lyon money channel to the letter's own clear frame

    Letter. Charles de Danzay, French envoy in Denmark, to the Cardinal of Lorraine. [Copenhagen], 27 Jan 1557 (the
    year style, 1557 or 1558 n.s., is unresolved). BnF fr.20140 f.35r-v, continuing on f.36r. No edition. The only
    contemporary letter without a decipherment among the four January 1557 letters (f.16, f.24 and f.30 carry one). Key:
    Tomokiyo 2026, built from those siblings.

    Reading (`reading.txt`: 638 cipher tokens, H 509 of which 61 are nulls, M 59, U 70; 152 clear-hand words.
    `reading_f36.txt`: 67 cipher tokens, H 37, M 26, U 4; 13 clear words). About 23 of 37 lines give continuous French.
    Clear-hand words are in CAPS:
    > V9-V10 "tenu DAULTRE PART le chancelier ... peult ... beaucoup"; V2 "chancel[l]i-r ... Danoys ... quelque"; V5
    > "[r]oyaume ... a promis"; V17-V19 "passions ... ET affections de ceulx desquels ... me ayder ... la resolution des
    > affaires ... pend"; V23 "de leur volonte"; V24 "en doubte"; V25-V28 "au mo[y]en ... de faire ... quelque ...
    > Augsbourg par ... les marchans de Lion ... car de Augsbourg on le pourra"; f.36r "a vec [q]ues peu de perte pour le
    > change ET EN TELLE SORTE VOUS SEREZ ASSEURÉ DE FAIRE ... paye[?]ment par deca"

    *The Danish chancellor can do much. Something concerning the kingdom was promised. Danzay depends on the goodwill of
    people whose will is still in doubt. He proposes a way of doing something through Augsburg and the merchants of Lyon,
    "with little loss on the exchange", so that the Cardinal can be sure of making a payment in Denmark.* R1-R4, R7, V1,
    V3, V4 and V29 do not read.

    Already known. Tomokiyo's key page and the three deciphered sibling letters of 10 and 27 January (manuscript, not
    printed). The clear-hand passages of this letter give its frame: "affin que vous peussiez congnoistre l'estat des
    affaires de ce royaulme"; "la promesse qui de long temps m'a esté faicte"; "attendant plus certain et expres
    commandement".

    What the cipher adds. The names and the mechanism: the chancellor as the man who counts, and a money transfer
    through Augsburg with Lyon merchants, at little loss on exchange, to pay something in Denmark.

    Moment. *(context)* January 1557. The Truce of Vaucelles had just broken down, and Henri II was back at war with
    Philip II. France was courting Christian III of Denmark. Danzay began a posting at Copenhagen that lasted into the
    1580s.

    Significance: confirms or adds detail to what the edition gives. There is no edition of this letter. Against its
    own clear text and the siblings, the cipher adds the channel and the chancellor, not a new policy.

  • What it is

    Read at grade H from the surviving cipher book; a second ledger copy of both found in mssEC 25. Thirteen printed volumes, the Butler and Fox correspondence, the Lincoln Papers and Google Books phrase searches print neither telegram. A blind second reader agrees on 96 percent of tokens.

    N4 (no prior decipherment located)

    What the passage says, and why it matters

    form and key only -- substance printed in Butler's reply and Fox's telegram to Ericsson; most words in clear on the Huntington page since 2018

    Letter. Gustavus V. Fox, Assistant Secretary of the Navy, to Maj. Gen. B. F. Butler at Fort Monroe. Sent from
    Washington, 21 Apr 1864, 9.30 PM, operator Geo. D. Sheldon. Huntington mssEC 19 p.49 (and mssEC 25 p.77), US
    Military Telegraph "Ciphers Sent" ledger. No print of this telegram. Most of its words (53 of 63) have been in clear on
    the Huntington's crowd transcription since 2018.

    Reading (`reading.md`; code-word tokens H 11, from Cipher No. 1, mssEC 41):
    > "For Maj Gen B. F. Butler. If you can block the channel [Roanoke] Inland [sic: Island] so she can not get in to
    > Pamlico Sound we will have some camels made in a few days to lighten the Tecumseh iron clad so she can cross the bar
    > at Hatteras [8] feet and protect all [South] of [Roanoke] Island. [signed] Asst [Secretary of Navy] Fox. How are you
    > brave youths"

    Already known. Butler's reply (OR I/33 p.279) answers it: "our obstructions and your camels". Fox's parallel
    telegram to Ericsson, sent ten minutes earlier, asks for camels to lift the Tecumseh over an 8-foot bar (ORN I/9 p.667).

    What the cipher adds. The code words: Roanoke, 8 feet, South, the signer's title. The substance was already
    known from the clear words and the printed reply.

    Moment. *(context)* The CSS Albemarle had just sunk USS Southfield and helped retake Plymouth, NC (19-20 April
    1864). Washington feared the ram would break into Pamlico Sound.

    Significance: form and key only, substance already printed.

    Dear Reference Services,

I run a small project that reads cipher passages in digitised letters, with each reading checked by a separate search for any prior decipherment. Two groups of items in your collections are involved, and I would like to hand you the results and ask whether they are of use.

- Eckert papers, mssEC 41 (the 1864 cipher book) and mssEC 19: two telegrams to General Butler of 21 and 22 April 1864, Fox to Butler and Meigs to Butler, read with the book's key. Their substance is in print from the clear copies (Official Records and Butler's correspondence); what we add is the cipher form, the key as a machine-readable table, and twenty 1864 ledger entries read with it. No prior decipherment of the two telegrams was located in the Official Records, Butler's and Lincoln's editions, the Decoding the Civil War project pages or the catalogues.
- Blathwayt papers addenda, mssBLA 186 and 191(a), Madrid and Port St Mary intelligence of 1728-29, read with a key rebuilt from seven items in the same run that carry contemporary decipherments (395 groups). The clear part of BLA 186 is printed by Rose (Marchmont Papers, 1831, ii 414-15) with the cipher shown only as "(Cypher.)"; the cipher gives an agent's request for orders and Pareti's report on Ripperda's escape. No prior decipherment located in the Huntington and HMC catalogues, Rose, or full-text search.

Every reading is regenerated by a script from our transcription and the key, and every token is graded (H from a key source, C from known plaintext, M uncertain).   • What it is

    Read with the key of Orange's 1562 cipher, recovered from a sibling letter's contemporary decipherment (WVO 74), as secret news that the Emperor had asked August to elect Maximilian King of the Romans in his own lifetime. Demandt's regest summarises only the clear text; August's minute in Dresden, with a 'Zettel', has not been seen and may carry the enclosure in clear.

    N4 (no prior decipherment located; AUDIT.md 'N4 decision (D2)', two audits); C 247, M 53 of 300 signs

    What the passage says, and why it matters

    confirms or adds detail -- the Spanish and Navarre rumours are printed from other senders; the cipher adds Orange's own judgement (cryptanalytic reading, 35% M)

    Letter. William of Orange to Elector August, Breda, 24 Oct 1561. Autograph postscript, partly in cipher. Dresden
    HStA Loc. 9941/3 f.266r-v, WVO 53. No edition (Japikse's edition ends in September 1561). WVO summary of the clear
    letter: safe arrival at Breda, news of Spain and France, dogs and ferrets sent.

    Reading (`reading_53.txt`; 364 tokens: S 238, M 126. Cryptanalytic: key recovered without a key source,
    matched control 99.3%):
    > "neiuuer zeittung hab ich itc malsnicht zuberzuschreiben dan das dem printzen zu hispanien seines hern vatters
    > schuuester ehlich uermahlet uuerden und hieruber diesse lande zu uuregieren khomen sollen und dan ein gemein
    > geschret ist ... uuolle der hertzog uon uandosmen sein konigreich nauarra mit der gute oder krieg uuiederholen
    > mrch dunck aber die uueil der konnig zu franckreich noch so iungist es uuerde keinen furgang geuuinnen"

    *Of new tidings I have nothing to write except that the Prince of Spain is to marry his father's sister and come to
    govern these lands. There is also a common rumour that the Duke of Vendôme will recover his kingdom of Navarre by
    agreement or by war. But I think that, the King of France being still so young, it will come to nothing.*

    Already known. Both rumours are in print from other senders: the 1561 talk of a match between Don Carlos and his
    aunt Juana, and Antoine de Bourbon's Navarre claim.

    What the cipher adds. Orange's own judgement on the Navarre rumour, and the fact that he passed the Spanish
    rumours to August in cipher.

    Moment. Orange married Anna of Saxony, August's niece, at Leipzig in August 1561 *(context for the date)*. The
    couple reached Breda on 4 October (Demandt's regest of letter 57). This postscript comes three weeks later, as the new
    in-law alliance begins its correspondence. Rumours that Don Carlos would come to govern the Netherlands matter here,
    because Orange sat on the Council of State.

    Significance: confirms or adds detail to what the edition gives. The news is known. What it adds is Orange's
    opinion. It is a cryptanalytic reading with 35% M, and the second page (about 90 signs) is not fully read.

    Who to tell

    resources@huygens.knaw.nl, cc info@huygens.knaw.nl Huygens: sent, awaiting reply

    Dear editors of the Correspondence of William of Orange,

I run a small project that reads cipher passages in published letter images, with each reading checked by a separate search for any prior decipherment. Six letters in your William of Orange database carry cipher that the database records as unread. We have read them in part, with keys taken from contemporary decipherments of sibling letters in the same database, and located no prior decipherment in the principal editions after a logged search. None changes the story the editions tell; each adds a detail or shows the secret channel.

- WVO 4610, 4611, 4616, Lodewijk van Nassau to Orange, 3 June and 2 July 1573 and 12 April 1574 (two days before Mookerheyde). Key from the decipherments of 4613 and 4615. The cipher gives Louis's side of exchanges whose replies Groen prints (Archives, 1st ser., IV): a proposal to convene the Electors and French contacts; recruiting, with an offer of twenty companies for Friesland; and, in 1574, march and lodging.
- WVO 53, Orange to Elector August, 24 Oct 1561. Read without any key source (a cryptanalytic result, 238 of 364 signs at grade S): news of the Prince of Spain's marriage and the Netherlands, Vendôme and Navarre, and Orange's own judgement that it would come to nothing. The rumours are printed from other senders.
- WVO 57, August to Orange, 18 Nov 1561, read with the key of Orange's 1562 cipher: the Emperor's approach about electing Maximilian King of the Romans, told to Orange in secret within days of the Palatine letters Kluckhohn prints.
- WVO 126, Orange to August, 16 Sept 1564, read with a key from WVO 98: the Queen of Spain's miscarriage, printed from Saint-Sulpice; the cipher adds only the channel.

Readings, keys, per-token grades and the full search log for each: https://github.com/noautopilot/cipher-lab/tree/main/ciphers/lodewijk-van-nassau-1573-74 and https://github.com/noautopilot/cipher-lab/tree/main/ciphers/august-van-saksen-1561-64 (AUDIT.md in each). Images are your own record pages (brief?nr=4610, 4611, 4616, 53, 57, 126). If your editors know a decipherment of any of these, in the KHA or Dresden minutes or elsewhere, we would be glad to cite it.

If useful, we can hand over a table of the 73 WVO letters we found with cipher passages, marked which an edition prints in clear and which stand unread, and the keys as machine-readable tables, in whatever format suits the database. And if there are unread passages you would most like read, name them and we will try those first.

## Reply (received 26 Sept 2026, dr. Ineke Huysman, Huygens Instituut; pasted by the person; recorded as data)

Observations, each with what it changes here:

1. **WVO 53 and 126: the KHA holds twentieth-century copies in the Collectie Japikse.** Japikse printed cipher passages deciphered in his first volume (1934); these copies were preparation for a continuation that never appeared. She judges them the most likely place for an earlier decipherment, more so than the editions. -> Both audits list the Japikse copy as an unseen witness (AUDIT.md V3 and A2). An editor of the database now names it the first place to look, so the "no prior decipherment located" sentence for 53 and 126 must carry that witness by name until it is seen; the N-class question is referred to the verifier lane (ROOM, 26 Sept). Access needs a person at the KHA: ASKS row 67. 2. **126 and 57: the minutes (KHA A11-XIVi-04-26 "met een 'Zeitung'"; Dresden Loc. 9941/3 f.268-269 "met een 'Zettel'") may simply carry the text in clear.** -> Already in AUDIT.md (2.3, A2.2 item 2); keep the sentence in every summary of the results. 3. **The Palatine audience in Kluckhohn (nr. 148) dates from 30-31 December 1561, six weeks after August's letter of 18 November, not within days.** -> The audit had the date right (AUDIT.md D2.1 row 1, V3c row 9); the sent email and N4-READINGS.md said "within days" -- both corrected 26 Sept 2026. 4. **53 rests entirely on a recovered key, with a third of the signs uncertain: present it as a proposal, not a text.** -> Adopted: the status.json row, CONTRIBUTIONS.md and every summary say "proposed reading" for 53. 5. Two questions before she takes it further: (a) who is responsible for the project and how the work is divided between people and AI agents -- "it should be stated plainly in any contact with editors"; (b) what we intend to do with the results (publication, deposit with the holding archives, or only the repository). -> The person answers both; the disclosure sentence is now a standing rule for every outreach draft (outreach/README.md).

  • What it is

    Continuous French on 32 of 55 lines; N4 (no prior decipherment located) after two audits and the final-families decision of 24 Sept 2026; outreach waits on the Gramont JSTOR rows. 24 Sept 2026 08:00 (LANE G2 worker E): two more values at grade S in the extended f.30 reading with a shuffled-position control (Tb=O, q=B); extended H1486 S181 M239 U63 of 1969; f.30r L12 now French with gaps, L01, L02, L11 still not.

    N4 (no prior decipherment located)

    What the passage says, and why it matters

    confirms or adds detail -- the readable cipher is procedure (a secret article routed to the king under cover), no political news

    Letter. Gabriel de Gramont, bishop of Tarbes (created cardinal 8 June 1530), to Jean Breton de Villandry, the
    king's secretary. Rome, 20 May [1530]. BnF fr.2980 f.29r, no.21 (Gallica ark btv1b9059991d). No printed edition of the
    letter. Its existence and date are in the 1868 BnF catalogue and the *Catalogue des actes de François Ier* IX.
    Mixed letter: about 7 clear lines, 14 cipher lines, clear signature. Key: Gramont 1530 (Tomokiyo; Lasry 2023).

    Reading (`reading.txt`; 568 tokens: H 533, M 30, U 5; one reconciled transcription). The solver's word division,
    lines 1-3 and 8-12:
    > "il y baille a ce porteur ... article que j'ay mis a part, et i[l?] faict l'adresse de dessus a vous, combien que ce
    > soit au roy ... et luy ay monstre tout re[com]tenu pour le contenter et oster ... de suspecon, qui est cause que j'ay
    > faict ledit article a part pour vous do[nn]er cognoissance de tout, mais je vous prie advertissement(?) ... ledict
    > seigneur et tous aultres ... soit mene ... secretement"

    *He has handed the bearer an article that he set apart, addressed on the outside to Villandry although it is meant
    for the king. He showed [someone] the whole content to satisfy him and remove suspicion, which is why he made the
    article separate: to let Villandry know everything. He asks that it be handled secretly.* Only L10-L11 and parts of
    L02, L08, L09 and L12 are continuous French. L01, L03-L07 and L13 have probable sign misidentifications. "du vingtiesme"
    (L07) fits the date.

    Already known. The clear part (grade M, read by eye): Gramont sends a copy of the letters he wrote "lundi" [16 May]
    in case this courier reaches Villandry before the packet to the king; "la satisfaction de nostre Sainct Pere"; "les
    affaires du Roy par deca". Tomokiyo named the letter as readable with the key, and Bourdeau catalogued it (no.328).
    Neither gave a reading.

    What the cipher adds. Procedure and secrecy. It shows a two-channel dispatch: an open text shown to a third party
    to allay suspicion, and a separate article for the king sent under cover to his secretary. That article is probably
    f.30 (an inference, grade I, in AUDIT.md). There is no political news in the readable part.

    Moment. Rome, May 1530 *(context)*: Charles V had been crowned at Bologna in February. The imperial and papal army
    had besieged Florence since October 1529. Francis I's sons were still hostages in Spain under the Treaty of Cambrai.
    Gramont was in Rome from April to June 1530 on the king's business and on Henry VIII's divorce. The consistory of
    8 June made him cardinal.

    Significance: confirms or adds detail to what the edition gives. It adds a detail of diplomatic practice, the
    routing of the secret article. The letter's substance lies in f.30.

    Who to tell

    Hello, and thank you for the catalogue. Your CATALOGUE.md line 76 lists these two letters as readable with the Gramont 1530 key you hold (gramont1529). We applied that key to both. What we found, in the wording of our audit:

- f.29r, no.21, to Villandry: "No prior decipherment located of Gabriel de Gramont's cipher letter to Jean Breton de Villandry, Rome, 20 May 1530 (BnF fr.2980 f.29r, no.21). Tomokiyo identified it as readable, and Bourdeau catalogued it (no.328). We read its 568-sign cipher passage in part with the Gramont 1530 key published by Tomokiyo and Lasry (2023): 533 signs at grade H, 30 uncertain, 5 unkeyed. The search log is in AUDIT.md."
- f.30r-v, no.22, to Francis I: "No prior decipherment located of Gabriel de Gramont's all-cipher letter to Francis I, Rome, 20 May 1530 (BnF fr.2980 f.30r-v, no.22). Tomokiyo identified it as readable, and Bourdeau catalogued it (no.328). We read it in part with the Gramont 1530 key published by Tomokiyo and Lasry (2023): of 1,973 signs, 1,500 are at grade H and 158 at grade S with a matched control. Its closest print is the sibling letter of April 1530 (Archivio storico italiano, Appendice I, pp.473-481), which is a different letter. The search log is in AUDIT.md."

Prior print this rests on: the key (Tomokiyo, "Gramont's Cipher (1530)"; Lasry's 2023 confirmation, https://cryptiana.web.fc2.com/code/GL.htm). The letters' existence and date are in the *Catalogue des actes de François Ier* IX, p.61 (https://archive.org/details/collectiondesord09acad).

Neither letter's plaintext was located in Camusat, Le Grand, the Catalogue des actes de François Ier (IX [412] records the letter), Bourdeau's project or full-text search.

No prior decipherment located in Ribier, Richard's 1910 monograph, the Danish editions, or Ryabov's 2025 article on Danzay's later cipher (Quaestio Rossica 13/4), which concerns his 1574 and 1578 letters.
- Correction: Raince to Madame, Rome, 25 Oct 1525 (Dupuy 452 ff.28-29), which your 1526 key opens, has its plaintext and a contemporary decipherment printed by Jacqueton (La politique extérieure de Louise de Savoie, 1892, pièce XXXIII, pp.366-370). If your list carries it as unsolved, that is the citation.

  • What it is

    A 569-sign cipher passage read with the published Gramont 1530 key: 533 signs valued after a blind second reader's corrections, several lines continuous French. No printed plaintext or decipherment located after two logged searches. The second leaf (f.30, 1,973 signs) now reads with the same key on 22 of 55 lines and is with the verifier.

    N4 (no prior decipherment located)

    What the passage says, and why it matters

    confirms or adds detail -- the readable cipher is procedure (a secret article routed to the king under cover), no political news

    Letter. Gabriel de Gramont, bishop of Tarbes (created cardinal 8 June 1530), to Jean Breton de Villandry, the
    king's secretary. Rome, 20 May [1530]. BnF fr.2980 f.29r, no.21 (Gallica ark btv1b9059991d). No printed edition of the
    letter. Its existence and date are in the 1868 BnF catalogue and the *Catalogue des actes de François Ier* IX.
    Mixed letter: about 7 clear lines, 14 cipher lines, clear signature. Key: Gramont 1530 (Tomokiyo; Lasry 2023).

    Reading (`reading.txt`; 568 tokens: H 533, M 30, U 5; one reconciled transcription). The solver's word division,
    lines 1-3 and 8-12:
    > "il y baille a ce porteur ... article que j'ay mis a part, et i[l?] faict l'adresse de dessus a vous, combien que ce
    > soit au roy ... et luy ay monstre tout re[com]tenu pour le contenter et oster ... de suspecon, qui est cause que j'ay
    > faict ledit article a part pour vous do[nn]er cognoissance de tout, mais je vous prie advertissement(?) ... ledict
    > seigneur et tous aultres ... soit mene ... secretement"

    *He has handed the bearer an article that he set apart, addressed on the outside to Villandry although it is meant
    for the king. He showed [someone] the whole content to satisfy him and remove suspicion, which is why he made the
    article separate: to let Villandry know everything. He asks that it be handled secretly.* Only L10-L11 and parts of
    L02, L08, L09 and L12 are continuous French. L01, L03-L07 and L13 have probable sign misidentifications. "du vingtiesme"
    (L07) fits the date.

    Already known. The clear part (grade M, read by eye): Gramont sends a copy of the letters he wrote "lundi" [16 May]
    in case this courier reaches Villandry before the packet to the king; "la satisfaction de nostre Sainct Pere"; "les
    affaires du Roy par deca". Tomokiyo named the letter as readable with the key, and Bourdeau catalogued it (no.328).
    Neither gave a reading.

    What the cipher adds. Procedure and secrecy. It shows a two-channel dispatch: an open text shown to a third party
    to allay suspicion, and a separate article for the king sent under cover to his secretary. That article is probably
    f.30 (an inference, grade I, in AUDIT.md). There is no political news in the readable part.

    Moment. Rome, May 1530 *(context)*: Charles V had been crowned at Bologna in February. The imperial and papal army
    had besieged Florence since October 1529. Francis I's sons were still hostages in Spain under the Treaty of Cambrai.
    Gramont was in Rome from April to June 1530 on the king's business and on Henry VIII's divorce. The consistory of
    8 June made him cardinal.

    Significance: confirms or adds detail to what the edition gives. It adds a detail of diplomatic practice, the
    routing of the secret article. The letter's substance lies in f.30.

    Who to tell

  • What it is

    LANE AX (owner account) round 3: the 1574 table plus name codes recovered from the 5550 interlinear gloss and the 4614 companion decipherment fill two of the six passages (a third, 172, is under its own audit) Groen could not decipher in 5797; the 4610/4611/4616 unread tokens fell from 541 to 320 under the merged key.

    N4 after two audits (V8-NA5797 04:09, V8-NA5797-2 04:41 UTC) on the two blanks Groen IV no. CDXLIV pp.223-225 left undeciphered: 'Landgraf' (161) and 'Pfaltzgraf' (153), grade H each, valued from a clean period interlinear gloss on the sibling 5550 and located with the Lodewijk 1574 table (ours, aligned from sibling decipherments); letter text N0 (printed by Groen); key_full v3 shows 0 regressions on the 4613/4615 controls and two fresh-instance re-derivations were byte-identical; V8-QA7 (window 03:08-05:44) clean on the lodewijk items; gate 2: open-index, Google Books and JSTOR rows 88-91 answered, no relevant hit. A third blank (172, p6) is kept out of this row: V8-NA172 classed it N4, the second audit V8-NA172-2 lowered it to N2 (Lossen 1882 p.212 prints the fact, not the blank), and V9-NA172-3 (26 Sept 08:06 UTC) withdrew it from classification (AUDIT.md A4: three period values, none licensed for 5797), and the code's value is contested -- le Conte Jean by the 4614 decipherment, le Conte Louis by the 5801 period gloss (AX2-5801ADJ), so the token stays grade M with no licensed value; 4610/4611/4616 stay N4 after the key revision (V8.3)

    What the passage says, and why it matters

    confirms or adds detail -- Orange's printed reply carries the substance; fragments add Louis's proposals (convocation of Electors, French contacts)

    Letter. Louis of Nassau to his brother William of Orange. 3 June 1573, no place ("Pour Hollande", duplicate).
    KHA A 11/XIV D/13a, WVO 4610. No edition. Orange's reply of 17 June 1573 (Leiden) is printed: Groen van Prinsterer,
    *Archives* I/IV, Lettre CDXXVII, pp.156-160.

    Reading (`reading_4610.txt`; 1,545 tokens including clear words: C 1,098, M 101, I 58, U 288. Numerals only:
    1,222, C 1,073). Key aligned from the contemporary decipherments of 4613/4615. Words in braces are clear text or word
    signs; lower-case runs are decoded numerals with no word breaks. Readable fragments:
    > "Il fault que [vous?] pardonne de ce ... qui depesche ... ce lundy"; "de la rechute"; "semployer pour rapaiser";
    > "electeurs ... je luy ay propose une conuocation ... des electeurs"; "aduisez aux conditions ... proposees ... affin
    > [de] pouuoir ouurir ... la negotiation"; "leduc dalencon"; "trois cens cheuaux"; "capiteines frencois";
    > "strabourg"; "delapaix"

    *An apology for a delayed messenger. His relapse [of fever]. Efforts to appease. He proposed a convocation of the
    Electors and asks Orange's view of the conditions, so as to open a negotiation. The Duke of Alençon, 300 horse, French
    captains, Strasbourg, peace.* Most lines are fragmentary.

    Already known. Orange's reply answers it: "Vostre lettre du 3e jour de ce mois ... la fiebvre vous a de rechief
    quicté", the Emperor's answer to the princes about pacification, and three cavalry companies.

    What the cipher adds. Louis's side of it: the proposal to convene the Electors, and the French contacts (Alençon,
    French captains), which the reply only answers.

    Moment. *(context)* In June 1573 Haarlem was under siege (it fell on 12 July). Louis was in Germany seeking
    princely and French backing and a mediated peace. Orange held Holland and Zeeland alone.

    Significance: confirms or adds detail to what the edition gives. The printed reply carries the substance. The
    fragments add Louis's proposals.

    Who to tell

    resources@huygens.knaw.nl, cc info@huygens.knaw.nl Huygens: sent, awaiting reply

    One note covers 2 readings (lodewijk-van-nassau-1573-74, august-van-saksen-1561-64); send it once and tick it once.

    sent 24 Sept 2026 (by the person); reply received 26 Sept 2026 from dr. E.C.M. (Ineke) Huysman, senior onderzoeker NL-Lab, Huygens Instituut -- see 'Reply' below; answer drafted in outreach/huygens-reply-2026-09-26.md for the person to send

    To
    resources@huygens.knaw.nl, cc info@huygens.knaw.nl
    Subject
    Cipher passages in six WVO letters (4610, 4611, 4616; 53, 57, 126) read with contemporary keys
    Body
    Dear editors of the Correspondence of William of Orange,

I run a small project that reads cipher passages in published letter images, with each reading checked by a separate search for any prior decipherment. Six letters in your William of Orange database carry cipher that the database records as unread. We have read them in part, with keys taken from contemporary decipherments of sibling letters in the same database, and located no prior decipherment in the principal editions after a logged search. None changes the story the editions tell; each adds a detail or shows the secret channel.

- WVO 4610, 4611, 4616, Lodewijk van Nassau to Orange, 3 June and 2 July 1573 and 12 April 1574 (two days before Mookerheyde). Key from the decipherments of 4613 and 4615. The cipher gives Louis's side of exchanges whose replies Groen prints (Archives, 1st ser., IV): a proposal to convene the Electors and French contacts; recruiting, with an offer of twenty companies for Friesland; and, in 1574, march and lodging.
- WVO 53, Orange to Elector August, 24 Oct 1561. Read without any key source (a cryptanalytic result, 238 of 364 signs at grade S): news of the Prince of Spain's marriage and the Netherlands, Vendôme and Navarre, and Orange's own judgement that it would come to nothing. The rumours are printed from other senders.
- WVO 57, August to Orange, 18 Nov 1561, read with the key of Orange's 1562 cipher: the Emperor's approach about electing Maximilian King of the Romans, told to Orange in secret within days of the Palatine letters Kluckhohn prints.
- WVO 126, Orange to August, 16 Sept 1564, read with a key from WVO 98: the Queen of Spain's miscarriage, printed from Saint-Sulpice; the cipher adds only the channel.

Readings, keys, per-token grades and the full search log for each: https://github.com/noautopilot/cipher-lab/tree/main/ciphers/lodewijk-van-nassau-1573-74 and https://github.com/noautopilot/cipher-lab/tree/main/ciphers/august-van-saksen-1561-64 (AUDIT.md in each). Images are your own record pages (brief?nr=4610, 4611, 4616, 53, 57, 126). If your editors know a decipherment of any of these, in the KHA or Dresden minutes or elsewhere, we would be glad to cite it.

    What the passage says, and why it matters

    confirms or adds detail -- Orange's printed reply carries the substance; fragments add Louis's proposals (convocation of Electors, French contacts)

    Letter. Louis of Nassau to his brother William of Orange. 3 June 1573, no place ("Pour Hollande", duplicate).
    KHA A 11/XIV D/13a, WVO 4610. No edition. Orange's reply of 17 June 1573 (Leiden) is printed: Groen van Prinsterer,
    *Archives* I/IV, Lettre CDXXVII, pp.156-160.

    Reading (`reading_4610.txt`; 1,545 tokens including clear words: C 1,098, M 101, I 58, U 288. Numerals only:
    1,222, C 1,073). Key aligned from the contemporary decipherments of 4613/4615. Words in braces are clear text or word
    signs; lower-case runs are decoded numerals with no word breaks. Readable fragments:
    > "Il fault que [vous?] pardonne de ce ... qui depesche ... ce lundy"; "de la rechute"; "semployer pour rapaiser";
    > "electeurs ... je luy ay propose une conuocation ... des electeurs"; "aduisez aux conditions ... proposees ... affin
    > [de] pouuoir ouurir ... la negotiation"; "leduc dalencon"; "trois cens cheuaux"; "capiteines frencois";
    > "strabourg"; "delapaix"

    *An apology for a delayed messenger. His relapse [of fever]. Efforts to appease. He proposed a convocation of the
    Electors and asks Orange's view of the conditions, so as to open a negotiation. The Duke of Alençon, 300 horse, French
    captains, Strasbourg, peace.* Most lines are fragmentary.

    Already known. Orange's reply answers it: "Vostre lettre du 3e jour de ce mois ... la fiebvre vous a de rechief
    quicté", the Emperor's answer to the princes about pacification, and three cavalry companies.

    What the cipher adds. Louis's side of it: the proposal to convene the Electors, and the French contacts (Alençon,
    French captains), which the reply only answers.

    Moment. *(context)* In June 1573 Haarlem was under siege (it fell on 12 July). Louis was in Germany seeking
    princely and French backing and a mediated peace. Orange held Holland and Zeeland alone.

    Significance: confirms or adds detail to what the edition gives. The printed reply carries the substance. The
    fragments add Louis's proposals.

  • What it is

    Birch prints the cipher and no decipherment. Read with the key given by Birch's printed decipherments of Stamford's letters of 20 and 30 March 1655; Thurloe's office deciphered this system in 1655 and Tomokiyo has reconstructed and published it, and our table agrees with his on every value in this letter. H 64, C 338, M 16, U 6 of 424; eleven lines of p.188 still partly incoherent. No prior decipherment of this letter located in Birch, BHO, CSPD 1655, the Clarendon calendar, Nicholas Papers, Abbott, Clarke Papers, Gardiner, Underdown, the Bodleian catalogues, Tomokiyo's pages, the solver repositories, DECODE, Google Books or IA full text (24 Sept 2026). The original leaf, Bodleian MS Rawl. A. 24, has not been seen.

    N4 (no prior decipherment located); two audits

    What the passage says, and why it matters

    confirms or adds detail -- Birch prints the letter and its endorsement, and Stamford's deciphered 20 and 30 March letters cover the same rising; P4 adds the earliest warning and its particulars

    Letter. William Stamford, a Royalist offering his services, from Calais, 13 March 1655 N.S. (endorsed "13/3 March
    1654/5"). Recipient unnamed. Macray's Rawlinson index points to Col. Thomas Kelsey. "Your friend" is the Protector.
    Bodleian MS Rawl. A. 24 (leaf unseen). Printed: Birch, *Thurloe State Papers* III (1742) pp.187-189, with the
    cipher as numerals and no decipherment, and also on British History Online. The endorsement is printed: "His desire of a
    correspondence, and promise of performing some emminent service (in case my lord protector will engage to reward him)
    namely in discovering of the plott, &c."

    Reading (`pool_1654/reading_P4.txt`: 424 tokens, H 64, C 338, S 0, M 16, U 6). The key comes from Birch's
    printed decipherments of Stamford's letters of 20 and 30 March 1655 (cross-letter control 92-94%). The runs are
    mechanical; my restorations follow in italics:
    > "it is come to my knowledge by {meerechance} and {withouttheleastiniunctionof} secrecy {thepersonigoti(q?)o(w?)tfrom}
    > taking it {forgranted} upon his meeting {meeasheethoug(x?)t} stealing out of whitehall {<england?>} that i was not only
    > perticularly informed of {thematerbutalso} employed in it yet since it is the absolute breaking of
    > {apothhathaibeeneahtching} by {thewholepartieof_} ... render my selfe {irreconciiablkwiththemaslongaailite}"
    > ... "the {qualitie} i am of the familiar acquaintance i have amongst all the {<thecavaliers?>}" ... "it is a more
    > {<generalrising>} ... in which more {peopleareengagedtheninanioneof} the {formerones(w?)hatsoeutr} ...
    > {<have>notla} ... {de<designs>tosurp} ... thousand armes {<which>l} ... {readitobeemaceuse} and {whentheirebusines}
    > {eshalberipeforit} and which makes mee beleeve the time appointed for it is not farre off"

    *By chance, and under no promise of secrecy, he learned of a plot that "hath been a hatching" by the whole party of
    [143]. His source assumed he was in it. Revealing it will make him irreconcilable with them for life. He has a wide
    acquaintance among the cavaliers. It is a more general rising, with more people engaged than in any former one. There
    are designs to surprise [places], a thousand arms ready for use when their business is ripe, and the time appointed is
    not far off.* Eleven lines of p.188 (L51-61) stay partly incoherent. "England" and "the cavaliers" are M.

    Already known. The clear prose asks for a reward and protection and offers to come to London unseen. The
    endorsement summarises the offer. Stamford's later letters of 20 and 30 March are printed by Birch with their
    decipherment and also speak of the "general rising". Tomokiyo has since reconstructed the system. Our table agrees with his on every value that occurs in this letter.

    What the cipher adds. The substance behind the endorsement's "plott": a warning dated 3 March O.S. that a general
    Royalist rising was imminent, bigger than earlier ones, with arms ready. It is the earliest of Stamford's warnings in
    the printed series.

    Moment. The Royalist rising of March 1655 *(context for the dates)*: the general rising was planned for 8 March
    O.S. and fizzled almost everywhere. Penruddock's party seized Salisbury on 12 March O.S. and was beaten at South Molton
    on 14 March O.S. Stamford wrote about a week before. The Protectorate's foreknowledge of the rising from informers is
    well known. This letter is one more such source, not the first.

    Significance: confirms or adds detail to what the edition gives. Birch prints the letter and its endorsement, and
    Stamford's deciphered letters of 20 and 30 March cover the same rising. P4 adds the earliest warning and its
    particulars (scale, a thousand arms, imminence).

Dear Special Collections team,

I run a small open project on unsolved historical ciphers; part of it is the printed cipher letters in Thurloe's State Papers. I direct it and send its letters myself; AI agents (Claude models) do the reading, the edition searches and the checking, every step logged in the repository linked below. For each letter we check whether anyone has deciphered it before.

I would like to order a reproduction of one leaf, or a low-resolution reference copy if that is simpler: William Stamford's letter from Calais of 13 March 1655, which Birch printed in cipher in Thurloe's State Papers, vol. iii, pp.187-189, with the margin reference "Vol. xxiv. p.76" (and p.73 for the covering letter printed with it). That volume is now MSS. Rawl. A. 24/1-2; part 1 ("Thurloe Papers, vol. xxiv, part 1", MS. Rawl. A. 24/1) is ark:/29072/x0x920fw11hz, marked "Not available online" in your catalogue and described folio by folio in item-level records. None of the item records we could see names Stamford or Calais, and we have not been able to work out from those records which modern folio carries Birch's old pp.73 and 76. Could your staff resolve that from the item list, or should I simply submit a reproduction request quoting the old pagination?

What I hope to learn from the leaf is whether Thurloe's office wrote or filed a decipherment beside the cipher. We have found no earlier decipherment of this letter after a logged search; an image would either confirm that or show us an office decipherment we have missed.

Everything we have on the letter, including the search log, is at https://github.com/NoAutopilot/cipher-lab/tree/main/ciphers/thurloe-printed

    Hello. Your `thurloe/` folder uses Stamford's key (stamford.jpg) against other intercepts. Stamford's own letter of 13 March 1655, which Birch printed with its cipher numerals and no decipherment, reads with the key given by Birch's decipherments of his letters of 20 and 30 March. In the wording of our audit:

"Birch (1742) prints William Stamford's letter from Calais of 13 March 1655 with its cipher numerals and no decipherment. We read its cipher with the key given by Birch's printed decipherments of Stamford's letters of 20 and 30 March 1655. Thurloe's office deciphered this system in 1655, and Tomokiyo has since reconstructed and published it (Cryptiana, 'William Stamford (1655)'). Our table agrees with his on every value that occurs in this letter. By token: H 64, C 338, M 16, U 6 of 424, and eleven lines of p.188 are still partly incoherent. No prior decipherment of this letter located in Birch and its indexes, British History Online, CSPD 1655, the Clarendon calendar vol. 3, the Nicholas Papers, Abbott, the Clarke Papers, Gardiner, Underdown, the Bodleian's catalogues, Tomokiyo's pages, the solver repositories, DECODE, Google Books or Internet Archive full text (searched 24 Sept 2026, AUDIT.md). The original leaf, Bodleian MS Rawl. A. 24, has not been seen."

Prior print this rests on: Birch, *A Collection of the State Papers of John Thurloe* vol. 3 (1742), pp.187-189 (the letter) and pp.274-280 (the decipherments that give the key), https://archive.org/details/collectionofstat03thur/page/187

- Transcription, key, reading (regenerated by script), per-token grades and search log:
https://github.com/noautopilot/cipher-lab/tree/main/ciphers/thurloe-printed
and https://github.com/noautopilot/cipher-lab/blob/main/ciphers/thurloe-printed/AUDIT.md (section "N4 set, P4")

The primary source is Birch's printed page; the manuscript leaf has not been imaged for us.

    Links a recipient can check

    folder audit reading edition

  • What it is

    The Orange-Saxony symbol ciphers use more than one key. System A (WVO 74, 1562) also reads August's enclosure of 18 Nov 1561 (WVO 57: the Emperor asked August to elect Maximilian King of the Romans). System B (98, 1563) reads 126 (1564: the Queen of Spain was bled and purged and lost the child). Orange's postscript of 24 Oct 1561 (WVO 53) was read cryptanalytically on both pages (Don Carlos marriage rumour; Vendôme and Navarre; the King of France too young for it to go forward). All three are N4: no prior decipherment located in the principal editions (Groen, Gachard, Rachfahl, von Weber, Kluckhohn; Japikse's edition ends Sept 1561), the KHA and WVO records or a phrase search (AUDIT.md 'N4 decision (D1)' and '(D2)'). Unseen witnesses may carry the text in clear: Orange's KHA minute of 126 with a 'Zeitung', August's Dresden minute of 57 with a 'Zettel'.

    126: C 214, M 26 of 240 signs, N4 (no prior decipherment located; AUDIT.md 'N4 decision (D1)'; KHA minute with a 'Zeitung' unimaged). 57: C 247, M 53 of 300, N4 (no prior decipherment located; AUDIT.md 'N4 decision (D2)', held on 'Re-check 57 against DNOK (V8)': WVO's DNOK is Demandt's regest nr. 113, HessJb 38 (1988), clear text only; Dresden minute with a 'Zettel' unseen). 53 (both pages): S 238, M 126 of 364, a cryptanalytic result, N4 (no prior decipherment located; AUDIT.md 'N4 decision (D2)'). Caveats from the WVO editor's reply (26 Sept 2026): the KHA Collectie Japikse copies of 53 and 126 (unseen) are the likeliest place for an earlier decipherment; the KHA minute of 126 ('Zeitung') and the Dresden minute of 57 ('Zettel') may carry the text in clear; Kluckhohn I nr. 148 is 30-31 Dec 1561, six weeks after 57. N-class of 53 and 126 referred to the verifier lane.

    What the passage says, and why it matters

    confirms or adds detail -- Elisabeth of Valois's miscarriage is printed (Saint-Sulpice, Cabié 1903); the cipher adds only that Orange passed it to Saxony

    Letter. Orange to Elector August, Brussels, 16 Sept 1564. Cipher postscript on f.139. Dresden HStA Geheimer Rat,
    Locat 8510/5 f.138r-140v, WVO 126. No edition. The WVO summary of the clear letter says that Wilhelm of Hesse reports
    August well, despite rumours of a hunting accident. Orange's KHA minute is recorded "met een 'Zeitung'" and is unimaged.

    Reading (`reading_126.txt`; 240 tokens: C 214, M 26; key aligned from WVO 98 and its contemporary decipherment):
    > "[wir] konnen auch [E.L.] in freundtlichem uertrauen nit uerhalten, [das] [wir] seidhero auss hispanien andere
    > zeitung bekommen haben, welche uermelden, [das] [die] konnigin so heftig kranc [ge]worden sei, [das] man ir [die]
    > adern zwei mahl slagen [und] auch zwei mahl purgieren mussen, dermassen [das] sie irer frucht erlediget worden sei"

    *We must tell Your Grace in confidence that we have since had other news from Spain: the Queen has been so gravely
    ill that she was bled twice and purged twice, so that she lost the child.*

    Already known. Elisabeth of Valois's illness and miscarriage in August 1564 are printed. For example,
    Saint-Sulpice to L'Aubespine, 19 Aug 1564, in Cabié, *Ambassade en Espagne* (1903) pp.289-290, reports fever, "une ou
    deux saignées", "les purgations", and that she has miscarried of two girls.

    What the cipher adds. That Orange sent the news on to Saxony, in cipher, about a month after the event.

    Moment. *(context)* In 1564 Orange was in Brussels at the height of the opposition to Granvelle's successors. The
    health of Philip II's queen, and so the Spanish succession, was a political subject. The link to Saxony runs through
    Anna.

    Significance: confirms or adds detail to what the edition gives. The event is well printed. The cipher adds only
    the channel.

    Who to tell

    resources@huygens.knaw.nl, cc info@huygens.knaw.nl Huygens: sent, awaiting reply

    To
    resources@huygens.knaw.nl, cc info@huygens.knaw.nl
    Subject
    Cipher passages in six WVO letters (4610, 4611, 4616; 53, 57, 126) read with contemporary keys
    Body
    Dear editors of the Correspondence of William of Orange,

I run a small project that reads cipher passages in published letter images, with each reading checked by a separate search for any prior decipherment. Six letters in your William of Orange database carry cipher that the database records as unread. We have read them in part, with keys taken from contemporary decipherments of sibling letters in the same database, and located no prior decipherment in the principal editions after a logged search. None changes the story the editions tell; each adds a detail or shows the secret channel.

- WVO 4610, 4611, 4616, Lodewijk van Nassau to Orange, 3 June and 2 July 1573 and 12 April 1574 (two days before Mookerheyde). Key from the decipherments of 4613 and 4615. The cipher gives Louis's side of exchanges whose replies Groen prints (Archives, 1st ser., IV): a proposal to convene the Electors and French contacts; recruiting, with an offer of twenty companies for Friesland; and, in 1574, march and lodging.
- WVO 53, Orange to Elector August, 24 Oct 1561. Read without any key source (a cryptanalytic result, 238 of 364 signs at grade S): news of the Prince of Spain's marriage and the Netherlands, Vendôme and Navarre, and Orange's own judgement that it would come to nothing. The rumours are printed from other senders.
- WVO 57, August to Orange, 18 Nov 1561, read with the key of Orange's 1562 cipher: the Emperor's approach about electing Maximilian King of the Romans, told to Orange in secret within days of the Palatine letters Kluckhohn prints.
- WVO 126, Orange to August, 16 Sept 1564, read with a key from WVO 98: the Queen of Spain's miscarriage, printed from Saint-Sulpice; the cipher adds only the channel.

Readings, keys, per-token grades and the full search log for each: https://github.com/noautopilot/cipher-lab/tree/main/ciphers/lodewijk-van-nassau-1573-74 and https://github.com/noautopilot/cipher-lab/tree/main/ciphers/august-van-saksen-1561-64 (AUDIT.md in each). Images are your own record pages (brief?nr=4610, 4611, 4616, 53, 57, 126). If your editors know a decipherment of any of these, in the KHA or Dresden minutes or elsewhere, we would be glad to cite it.

If useful, we can hand over a table of the 73 WVO letters we found with cipher passages, marked which an edition prints in clear and which stand unread, and the keys as machine-readable tables, in whatever format suits the database. And if there are unread passages you would most like read, name them and we will try those first.

cipher-lab
https://github.com/NoAutopilot/cipher-lab

## Reply (received 26 Sept 2026, dr. Ineke Huysman, Huygens Instituut; pasted by the person; recorded as data)

Observations, each with what it changes here:

1. **WVO 53 and 126: the KHA holds twentieth-century copies in the Collectie Japikse.** Japikse printed cipher passages deciphered in his first volume (1934); these copies were preparation for a continuation that never appeared. She judges them the most likely place for an earlier decipherment, more so than the editions. -> Both audits list the Japikse copy as an unseen witness (AUDIT.md V3 and A2). An editor of the database now names it the first place to look, so the "no prior decipherment located" sentence for 53 and 126 must carry that witness by name until it is seen; the N-class question is referred to the verifier lane (ROOM, 26 Sept). Access needs a person at the KHA: ASKS row 67. 2. **126 and 57: the minutes (KHA A11-XIVi-04-26 "met een 'Zeitung'"; Dresden Loc. 9941/3 f.268-269 "met een 'Zettel'") may simply carry the text in clear.** -> Already in AUDIT.md (2.3, A2.2 item 2); keep the sentence in every summary of the results. 3. **The Palatine audience in Kluckhohn (nr. 148) dates from 30-31 December 1561, six weeks after August's letter of 18 November, not within days.** -> The audit had the date right (AUDIT.md D2.1 row 1, V3c row 9); the sent email and N4-READINGS.md said "within days" -- both corrected 26 Sept 2026. 4. **53 rests entirely on a recovered key, with a third of the signs uncertain: present it as a proposal, not a text.** -> Adopted: the status.json row, CONTRIBUTIONS.md and every summary say "proposed reading" for 53. 5. Two questions before she takes it further: (a) who is responsible for the project and how the work is divided between people and AI agents -- "it should be stated plainly in any contact with editors"; (b) what we intend to do with the results (publication, deposit with the holding archives, or only the repository). -> The person answers both; the disclosure sentence is now a standing rule for every outreach draft (outreach/README.md).

Hello, and thank you for the project. We checked your unsolved-ciphers repository and decode-catalog.csv (24-25 Sept 2026) for this shelfmark, ark and sender/recipient and found no entry, so this is a candidate addition.

BnF Espagnol 144 f.22r-22v (within the Espagnol 142-144 codex, TOME III; the BnF finding aid's item 11), an unsigned Spanish instruction to the baron (abbé) de Mercy, dated Barneton 6 June 1648, has been read in part by our own cryptanalysis, in the wording of our audit: "BnF Espagnol 144 f.22, an unsigned Spanish instruction to the abbé (Baron) de Mercy dated Barneton 6 June 1648, has been read in part by our own cryptanalysis (496 of 522 code tokens at grade S, 26 at M, none from a key or known plaintext). No prior decipherment of this leaf and no print of its plaintext were located after three logged searches (25-28 Sept 2026, this AUDIT.md) that now cover the principal editions, catalogues and project pages; internal or unpublished work is not excluded. The substance of the mission it sets out is in print: Lonchay, Cuvelier and Lefèvre, Correspondance de la Cour d'Espagne IV (1933) no. 183, p. 71, calendars Leopold Wilhelm's own dispatch to Philip IV of June 1648 (Brussels, Secrétairerie d'État et de Guerre, reg. 240, fol. 201) as reporting that someone had been asked to ask the Elector of Brandenburg whether 3,000 infantry could be raised in his states for a corps to operate in Flanders. The reading is a cryptanalytic result, not confirmed by a key or a clear copy."

Prior print this rests on: Lonchay, Cuvelier and Lefèvre, *Correspondance de la Cour d'Espagne* IV (1933) no. 183, p. 71 (above; the mission's substance, not the instruction's own text). A further, closer-dated but unprinted lead: Henri Lonchay, *La rivalité de la France et de l'Espagne aux Pays-Bas (1635-1700)* (1896), p.445 n.2, cites an instruction to Mercy of 15 April 1648 (Brussels S.E.E. t. LXIV f.16) without printing it, cross-referencing ("Cf.") a further Leopold Wilhelm dispatch to the king of 30 August 1648 (t. LXV f.181) whose content it does not give:
https://archive.org/details/la-rivalite-de-la-france-et-d-espagne-aux-pays-bas-1635-1700/page/445

Dear Staatsarchiv Marburg staff,

I run a small open project on historical cipher letters. I direct it and send its letters myself; AI agents (Claude models) do the reading, the archival searches and the checking, every step logged in the repository linked below. We are working on the correspondence of Jan (Johann VI) van Nassau and Willem van Oranje, and are trying to trace a second copy of a letter of 17 April 1574 that Rolf Glawischnig cites, together with a letter of 21 April 1574 from Wesel, in his 1973 study Niederlande, Kalvinismus und Reichsgrafenstand 1559-1584. Nassau-Dillenburg unter Graf Johann VI (Marburg, 1973).

His footnote gives it as "STAMa 4f Nld. 165", which we read as the old fonds "4 f Niederlande", Paket or item 165. In Arcinsys that fonds now sits under HStAM, 4 f Staaten N (Staatenabteilung: Nassau-Nürnberg), 5 Nassau-Dillenburg, Fürsten, reorganised so that the old Paket numbers are no longer searchable fields, and we could not find item 165 by browsing the relevant subject nodes or by free-text search.

Could you tell me: 1. the current Arcinsys signature of the item Glawischnig cites as "4f Nld. 165"; 2. whether it is digitised (several neighbouring items in the same branch already have preview images online); 3. if it is, a link or a screen-quality image of the leaf or leaves dated 17 April 1574 (Jan van Nassau to Willem van Oranje, from Cologne), and in particular whether the two ciphered lines at the top of the letter's third page carry an interlinear or marginal decipherment of the period; 4. if it is not, what a reading-room photograph of the item would cost.

Full detail on the letter and what we have found so far is at https://github.com/NoAutopilot/cipher-lab/tree/main/ciphers/jan-van-nassau-1572-75

  • What it is

    The key sheet in the Condes de Linhares papers (ANTT PT/TT/CLNH/0086/11) says each number points to a page, column and word of 'o Diccionario'. LANE LX tested free scans against its worked example and found the book: A New Pocket Dictionary of the Portuguese and English Languages, abridged from Vieyra, Part I, London 1809; all twelve example groups land on the exact word. The two-page mid-letter fragment on m0002 then decodes 25 of 26 groups at grade H, one at M; pages 1 and 4 of the letter are not in the item, so no connected gloss is offered yet. A separate verifier classes both the reading and the identification N3. Safe sentence in AUDIT.md. On 25 Sept the Portuguese language judge, re-run on a corpus of the letter's own period (Correio Braziliense and O Investigador Portuguez, 1808-1819, 3.2 million letters), passes the reading (-1.044 against a real-text floor of -1.100; 192 of 200 genuine period windows pass the same gate), so the reading now counts; it stays below N4 because the edition most likely to print this letter, Coutinho's Textos Políticos, Económicos e Financeiros (Banco de Portugal, 1993), has not yet been read; a worker is on it.

    N3 (no prior decipherment located; AUDIT.md); H 24, M 2 of 26 groups (fresh re-derivation 24/26, 25 Sept); two audits agree at N3; language judge PASS on 25 Sept against a corpus of its own period (pt18, 1808-1819), with its control; check-solved still blocked until the sender-family edition (Textos Politicos 1993, local runner L10) is read

  • What it is

    Machine-readable Cipher No. 1 and No. 2 keys; every reading checked against print.

    H 20 of 20; 17 N1, 1 N1 (Lincoln), 2 N3

    What the passage says, and why it matters

    form and key only -- substance printed in Butler's reply and Fox's telegram to Ericsson; most words in clear on the Huntington page since 2018

    Letter. Gustavus V. Fox, Assistant Secretary of the Navy, to Maj. Gen. B. F. Butler at Fort Monroe. Sent from
    Washington, 21 Apr 1864, 9.30 PM, operator Geo. D. Sheldon. Huntington mssEC 19 p.49 (and mssEC 25 p.77), US
    Military Telegraph "Ciphers Sent" ledger. No print of this telegram. Most of its words (53 of 63) have been in clear on
    the Huntington's crowd transcription since 2018.

    Reading (`reading.md`; code-word tokens H 11, from Cipher No. 1, mssEC 41):
    > "For Maj Gen B. F. Butler. If you can block the channel [Roanoke] Inland [sic: Island] so she can not get in to
    > Pamlico Sound we will have some camels made in a few days to lighten the Tecumseh iron clad so she can cross the bar
    > at Hatteras [8] feet and protect all [South] of [Roanoke] Island. [signed] Asst [Secretary of Navy] Fox. How are you
    > brave youths"

    Already known. Butler's reply (OR I/33 p.279) answers it: "our obstructions and your camels". Fox's parallel
    telegram to Ericsson, sent ten minutes earlier, asks for camels to lift the Tecumseh over an 8-foot bar (ORN I/9 p.667).

    What the cipher adds. The code words: Roanoke, 8 feet, South, the signer's title. The substance was already
    known from the clear words and the printed reply.

    Moment. *(context)* The CSS Albemarle had just sunk USS Southfield and helped retake Plymouth, NC (19-20 April
    1864). Washington feared the ram would break into Pamlico Sound.

    Significance: form and key only, substance already printed.

    Who to tell

    reference@huntington.org Huntington: sent, awaiting reply

    One note covers 2 readings (eckert-1864, huntington-blathwayt-madrid-1728); send it once and tick it once.

    sent 24 Sept 2026 (by the person; reply pending)

    To
    reference@huntington.org
    Subject
    Two Civil War telegrams in mssEC 19 and two Blathwayt cipher passages (mssBLA 186, 191) read from your collections
    Body
    Dear Reference Services,

I run a small project that reads cipher passages in digitised letters, with each reading checked by a separate search for any prior decipherment. Two groups of items in your collections are involved, and I would like to hand you the results and ask whether they are of use.

- Eckert papers, mssEC 41 (the 1864 cipher book) and mssEC 19: two telegrams to General Butler of 21 and 22 April 1864, Fox to Butler and Meigs to Butler, read with the book's key. Their substance is in print from the clear copies (Official Records and Butler's correspondence); what we add is the cipher form, the key as a machine-readable table, and twenty 1864 ledger entries read with it. No prior decipherment of the two telegrams was located in the Official Records, Butler's and Lincoln's editions, the Decoding the Civil War project pages or the catalogues.
- Blathwayt papers addenda, mssBLA 186 and 191(a), Madrid and Port St Mary intelligence of 1728-29, read with a key rebuilt from seven items in the same run that carry contemporary decipherments (395 groups). The clear part of BLA 186 is printed by Rose (Marchmont Papers, 1831, ii 414-15) with the cipher shown only as "(Cypher.)"; the cipher gives an agent's request for orders and Pareti's report on Ripperda's escape. No prior decipherment located in the Huntington and HMC catalogues, Rose, or full-text search.

Every reading is regenerated by a script from our transcription and the key, and every token is graded (H from a key source, C from known plaintext, M uncertain). Folders with the keys, readings and the full search log: https://github.com/noautopilot/cipher-lab/tree/main/ciphers/eckert-1864 and https://github.com/noautopilot/cipher-lab/tree/main/ciphers/huntington-blathwayt-madrid-1728 (AUDIT.md in each). If the Library or the project already holds a decipherment of any of these, we would be glad to know and will correct our notes.

If it would help, we can supply the mssEC 41 key, the ledger readings and the Blathwayt key in whatever format your catalogue or the Decoding the Civil War project takes, and try any cipher books, telegrams or letters in your collections you would like read the same way.

cipher-lab
https://github.com/NoAutopilot/cipher-lab

  • What it is

    The British Library's catalogue does not say that Add MS 6912 (seven pages of numerals, each signed C.R. and countersigned George Digby) is the cipher Charles Wheatstone deciphered and printed in 1862 for the Philobiblon Society, 'Instructions pour le Sieur de Goffe' on the 1644 Orange marriage proposal. A check-solved worker made the identification from Ellis's 1858 description and Wheatstone's covering letter, and a separate verifier confirmed it against the British Museum's 1849 Index of Additional Manuscripts (6912 is the only Charles I cipher paper in the 1783-1835 accessions) and found the instructions themselves printed in clear from the Orange side by Groen van Prinsterer in 1859. Class N0: nothing here is new to the world, only to the shelfmark; the contribution is the shelfmark-to-publication link, which the verifier's AUDIT.md gives with its search log.

    N0 (AUDIT.md, verifier V6-DIGBY); key published (Wheatstone 1862, credited); text known (Groen van Prinsterer 1859, 2e serie IV pp.101-104)

Our reading of the letter layer, made before the verifier found Torpadie, is an independent re-decipherment: control 98.0% on letter 604 in the same layout; target graded S 665, M 29, U 77 (NOTES.md, R3 section).

    N0

  • What it is

    Its duplicate in the same collection, mssDE 108(B), carries Destouches's own 1781 decipherment, and a copy is in AAE CP États-Unis vol. 15 (cited in Idzerda, Lafayette Papers 3:319). Our reading, aligned to 108(B)'s decipherment, is now C 410, M 163, U 146 of 719 (was C 347 from the sibling letters' decipherments alone); found by the verifier before any further solver time. mssDE 68 is also N0 (its own complete decipherment).

    found-solved, N0 (AUDIT.md)

    Subject
    Gramont 1530 (fr.2980 ff.29-30) and Danzay 1557 (fr.20140 ff.35-36) read with your keys
    Body
    Dear Mr Tomokiyo,

Following the Bowes fragments, three items read with keys you published, and one correction for your list.

- BnF fr.2980 f.29r (no.21) and f.30r-v (no.22), Gramont to Villandry and to Francis I, Rome, 20 May 1530, read with the Gramont 1530 key you and George Lasry published. f.29r: 533 of 568 signs at grade H, a secret article routed to the king under cover; f.30: the runs continue the April plan for Florence, one run still unread. Neither letter's plaintext was located in Camusat, Le Grand, the Catalogue des actes de François Ier (IX [412] records the letter), Bourdeau's project or full-text search.
- BnF fr.20140 f.35r-36r, Danzay to the Cardinal of Lorraine, Copenhagen, 27 Jan 1557, read with your 2026 key reconstruction. No prior decipherment located in Ribier, Richard's 1910 monograph, the Danish editions, or Ryabov's 2025 article on Danzay's later cipher (Quaestio Rossica 13/4), which concerns his 1574 and 1578 letters.
- Correction: Raince to Madame, Rome, 25 Oct 1525 (Dupuy 452 ff.28-29), which your 1526 key opens, has its plaintext and a contemporary decipherment printed by Jacqueton (La politique extérieure de Louise de Savoie, 1892, pièce XXXIII, pp.366-370). If your list carries it as unsolved, that is the citation.

Keys, readings, per-token grades and the search logs:
https://github.com/noautopilot/cipher-lab/tree/main/ciphers/fr2980-gramont,
https://github.com/noautopilot/cipher-lab/tree/main/ciphers/fr20140-danzay-1557 and https://github.com/noautopilot/cipher-lab/tree/main/ciphers/dupuy452-carpi-1520 (AUDIT.md in each). Please use any of it with whatever credit you think right; corrections are welcome, especially where the key and the image disagree.

We hold an index of the published keys (yours, Lasry's and the two open solver projects') with the letters each has read, and can run them against Gallica volumes not yet checked. If there are items on your unsolved list you would most like attempted, we would be glad to take those first.

With thanks, cipher-lab
https://github.com/NoAutopilot/cipher-lab
