    folder audit

  • What it is

    Birch printed these letters in 1742 with their contemporary decipherment; we aligned it to the groups, which gives group-level keys for the Blake, Stamford and Fauconberg ciphers and real-data benchmarks.

    N0 (P3 body only; its postscript is open)

    What the passage says, and why it matters

    confirms or adds detail -- Birch prints the letter and its endorsement, and Stamford's deciphered 20 and 30 March letters cover the same rising; P4 adds the earliest warning and its particulars

    Letter. William Stamford, a Royalist offering his services, from Calais, 13 March 1655 N.S. (endorsed "13/3 March
    1654/5"). Recipient unnamed. Macray's Rawlinson index points to Col. Thomas Kelsey. "Your friend" is the Protector.
    Bodleian MS Rawl. A. 24 (leaf unseen). Printed: Birch, *Thurloe State Papers* III (1742) pp.187-189, with the
    cipher as numerals and no decipherment, and also on British History Online. The endorsement is printed: "His desire of a
    correspondence, and promise of performing some emminent service (in case my lord protector will engage to reward him)
    namely in discovering of the plott, &c."

    Reading (`pool_1654/reading_P4.txt`: 424 tokens, H 64, C 338, S 0, M 16, U 6). The key comes from Birch's
    printed decipherments of Stamford's letters of 20 and 30 March 1655 (cross-letter control 92-94%). The runs are
    mechanical; my restorations follow in italics:
    > "it is come to my knowledge by {meerechance} and {withouttheleastiniunctionof} secrecy {thepersonigoti(q?)o(w?)tfrom}
    > taking it {forgranted} upon his meeting {meeasheethoug(x?)t} stealing out of whitehall {<england?>} that i was not only
    > perticularly informed of {thematerbutalso} employed in it yet since it is the absolute breaking of
    > {apothhathaibeeneahtching} by {thewholepartieof_} ... render my selfe {irreconciiablkwiththemaslongaailite}"
    > ... "the {qualitie} i am of the familiar acquaintance i have amongst all the {<thecavaliers?>}" ... "it is a more
    > {<generalrising>} ... in which more {peopleareengagedtheninanioneof} the {formerones(w?)hatsoeutr} ...
    > {<have>notla} ... {de<designs>tosurp} ... thousand armes {<which>l} ... {readitobeemaceuse} and {whentheirebusines}
    > {eshalberipeforit} and which makes mee beleeve the time appointed for it is not farre off"

    *By chance, and under no promise of secrecy, he learned of a plot that "hath been a hatching" by the whole party of
    [143]. His source assumed he was in it. Revealing it will make him irreconcilable with them for life. He has a wide
    acquaintance among the cavaliers. It is a more general rising, with more people engaged than in any former one. There
    are designs to surprise [places], a thousand arms ready for use when their business is ripe, and the time appointed is
    not far off.* Eleven lines of p.188 (L51-61) stay partly incoherent. "England" and "the cavaliers" are M.

    Already known. The clear prose asks for a reward and protection and offers to come to London unseen. The
    endorsement summarises the offer. Stamford's later letters of 20 and 30 March are printed by Birch with their
    decipherment and also speak of the "general rising". Tomokiyo has since reconstructed the system. Our table agrees with his on every value that occurs in this letter.

    What the cipher adds. The substance behind the endorsement's "plott": a warning dated 3 March O.S. that a general
    Royalist rising was imminent, bigger than earlier ones, with arms ready. It is the earliest of Stamford's warnings in
    the printed series.

    Moment. The Royalist rising of March 1655 *(context for the dates)*: the general rising was planned for 8 March
    O.S. and fizzled almost everywhere. Penruddock's party seized Salisbury on 12 March O.S. and was beaten at South Molton
    on 14 March O.S. Stamford wrote about a week before. The Protectorate's foreknowledge of the rising from informers is
    well known. This letter is one more such source, not the first.

    Significance: confirms or adds detail to what the edition gives. Birch prints the letter and its endorsement, and
    Stamford's deciphered letters of 20 and 30 March cover the same rising. P4 adds the earliest warning and its
    particulars (scale, a thousand arms, imminence).

Dear Special Collections team,

I run a small open project on unsolved historical ciphers; part of it is the printed cipher letters in Thurloe's State Papers. I direct it and send its letters myself; AI agents (Claude models) do the reading, the edition searches and the checking, every step logged in the repository linked below. For each letter we check whether anyone has deciphered it before.

I would like to order a reproduction of one leaf, or a low-resolution reference copy if that is simpler: William Stamford's letter from Calais of 13 March 1655, which Birch printed in cipher in Thurloe's State Papers, vol. iii, pp.187-189, with the margin reference "Vol. xxiv. p.76" (and p.73 for the covering letter printed with it). That volume is now MSS. Rawl. A. 24/1-2; part 1 ("Thurloe Papers, vol. xxiv, part 1", MS. Rawl. A. 24/1) is ark:/29072/x0x920fw11hz, marked "Not available online" in your catalogue and described folio by folio in item-level records. None of the item records we could see names Stamford or Calais, and we have not been able to work out from those records which modern folio carries Birch's old pp.73 and 76. Could your staff resolve that from the item list, or should I simply submit a reproduction request quoting the old pagination?

What I hope to learn from the leaf is whether Thurloe's office wrote or filed a decipherment beside the cipher. We have found no earlier decipherment of this letter after a logged search; an image would either confirm that or show us an office decipherment we have missed.

Everything we have on the letter, including the search log, is at https://github.com/NoAutopilot/cipher-lab/tree/main/ciphers/thurloe-printed

[SIGN-OFF]

    Subject
    Thurloe: Stamford's letter from Calais of 13 March 1655 (Birch III pp.187-189) read with the Stamford key
    Body
    Hello. Your `thurloe/` folder uses Stamford's key (stamford.jpg) against other intercepts. Stamford's own letter of 13 March 1655, which Birch printed with its cipher numerals and no decipherment, reads with the key given by Birch's decipherments of his letters of 20 and 30 March. In the wording of our audit:

"Birch (1742) prints William Stamford's letter from Calais of 13 March 1655 with its cipher numerals and no decipherment. We read its cipher with the key given by Birch's printed decipherments of Stamford's letters of 20 and 30 March 1655. Thurloe's office deciphered this system in 1655, and Tomokiyo has since reconstructed and published it (Cryptiana, 'William Stamford (1655)'). Our table agrees with his on every value that occurs in this letter. By token: H 64, C 338, M 16, U 6 of 424, and eleven lines of p.188 are still partly incoherent. No prior decipherment of this letter located in Birch and its indexes, British History Online, CSPD 1655, the Clarendon calendar vol. 3, the Nicholas Papers, Abbott, the Clarke Papers, Gardiner, Underdown, the Bodleian's catalogues, Tomokiyo's pages, the solver repositories, DECODE, Google Books or Internet Archive full text (searched 24 Sept 2026, AUDIT.md). The original leaf, Bodleian MS Rawl. A. 24, has not been seen."

Prior print this rests on: Birch, *A Collection of the State Papers of John Thurloe* vol. 3 (1742), pp.187-189 (the letter) and pp.274-280 (the decipherments that give the key), https://archive.org/details/collectionofstat03thur/page/187

- Transcription, key, reading (regenerated by script), per-token grades and search log:
https://github.com/noautopilot/cipher-lab/tree/main/ciphers/thurloe-printed
and https://github.com/noautopilot/cipher-lab/blob/main/ciphers/thurloe-printed/AUDIT.md (section "N4 set, P4")

    What the passage says, and why it matters

    confirms or adds detail -- Birch prints the letter and its endorsement, and Stamford's deciphered 20 and 30 March letters cover the same rising; P4 adds the earliest warning and its particulars

    Letter. William Stamford, a Royalist offering his services, from Calais, 13 March 1655 N.S. (endorsed "13/3 March

    *By chance, and under no promise of secrecy, he learned of a plot that "hath been a hatching" by the whole party of
    [143]. His source assumed he was in it. Revealing it will make him irreconcilable with them for life. He has a wide

    are designs to surprise [places], a thousand arms ready for use when their business is ripe, and the time appointed is
    not far off.* Eleven lines of p.188 (L51-61) stay partly incoherent. "England" and "the cavaliers" are M.

    endorsement summarises the offer. Stamford's later letters of 20 and 30 March are printed by Birch with their

    Royalist rising was imminent, bigger than earlier ones, with arms ready. It is the earliest of Stamford's warnings in
    the printed series.

    Moment. The Royalist rising of March 1655 *(context for the dates)*: the general rising was planned for 8 March
    O.S. and fizzled almost everywhere. Penruddock's party seized Salisbury on 12 March O.S. and was beaten at South Molton
    on 14 March O.S. Stamford wrote about a week before. The Protectorate's foreknowledge of the rising from informers is

    Stamford's deciphered letters of 20 and 30 March cover the same rising. P4 adds the earliest warning and its

Dear Special Collections team,

I run a small open project on unsolved historical ciphers; part of it is the printed cipher letters in Thurloe's State Papers. I direct it and send its letters myself; AI agents (Claude models) do the reading, the edition searches and the checking, every step logged in the repository linked below. For each letter we check whether anyone has deciphered it before.

I would like to order a reproduction of one leaf, or a low-resolution reference copy if that is simpler: William Stamford's letter from Calais of 13 March 1655, which Birch printed in cipher in Thurloe's State Papers, vol. iii, pp.187-189, with the margin reference "Vol. xxiv. p.76" (and p.73 for the covering letter printed with it). That volume is now MSS. Rawl. A. 24/1-2; part 1 ("Thurloe Papers, vol. xxiv, part 1", MS. Rawl. A. 24/1) is ark:/29072/x0x920fw11hz, marked "Not available online" in your catalogue and described folio by folio in item-level records. None of the item records we could see names Stamford or Calais, and we have not been able to work out from those records which modern folio carries Birch's old pp.73 and 76. Could your staff resolve that from the item list, or should I simply submit a reproduction request quoting the old pagination?

What I hope to learn from the leaf is whether Thurloe's office wrote or filed a decipherment beside the cipher. We have found no earlier decipherment of this letter after a logged search; an image would either confirm that or show us an office decipherment we have missed.

Everything we have on the letter, including the search log, is at https://github.com/NoAutopilot/cipher-lab/tree/main/ciphers/thurloe-printed

[SIGN-OFF]

Bourdeau issue: sent, awaiting reply

    posted 24 Sept 2026 on dbourdeau/cyphersolver (issue number to confirm from the owner)

    To
    https://github.com/dbourdeau/cyphersolver/issues/new?title=Thurloe%3A+Stamford%… (opens with title and body filled in; press Submit)
    Subject
    Thurloe: Stamford's letter from Calais of 13 March 1655 (Birch III pp.187-189) read with the Stamford key
    Body
    Hello. Your `thurloe/` folder uses Stamford's key (stamford.jpg) against other intercepts. Stamford's own letter of 13 March 1655, which Birch printed with its cipher numerals and no decipherment, reads with the key given by Birch's decipherments of his letters of 20 and 30 March. In the wording of our audit:

"Birch (1742) prints William Stamford's letter from Calais of 13 March 1655 with its cipher numerals and no decipherment. We read its cipher with the key given by Birch's printed decipherments of Stamford's letters of 20 and 30 March 1655. Thurloe's office deciphered this system in 1655, and Tomokiyo has since reconstructed and published it (Cryptiana, 'William Stamford (1655)'). Our table agrees with his on every value that occurs in this letter. By token: H 64, C 338, M 16, U 6 of 424, and eleven lines of p.188 are still partly incoherent. No prior decipherment of this letter located in Birch and its indexes, British History Online, CSPD 1655, the Clarendon calendar vol. 3, the Nicholas Papers, Abbott, the Clarke Papers, Gardiner, Underdown, the Bodleian's catalogues, Tomokiyo's pages, the solver repositories, DECODE, Google Books or Internet Archive full text (searched 24 Sept 2026, AUDIT.md). The original leaf, Bodleian MS Rawl. A. 24, has not been seen."

Prior print this rests on: Birch, *A Collection of the State Papers of John Thurloe* vol. 3 (1742), pp.187-189 (the letter) and pp.274-280 (the decipherments that give the key), https://archive.org/details/collectionofstat03thur/page/187

- Transcription, key, reading (regenerated by script), per-token grades and search log:
https://github.com/noautopilot/cipher-lab/tree/main/ciphers/thurloe-printed
and https://github.com/noautopilot/cipher-lab/blob/main/ciphers/thurloe-printed/AUDIT.md (section "N4 set, P4")

The primary source is Birch's printed page; the manuscript leaf has not been imaged for us.

    Links a recipient can check

    folder audit reading edition

  • What it is

    N0: both letters carry an interlinear decipherment on the leaf, in a second hand (probably contemporary), except nine groups on fol.262r that nothing reads; aligning it gives key_1696 (129 codes, grade C) that regenerates the reading. The folios are cited in print since 1879 (Boislisle's Saint-Simon, Mancel 1903); the deciphered text was not found in print. fol.249r is show-through, no cipher.

    C 584, M 82, U 1 of 667 groups (no H: no key sheet); novelty N0 (AUDIT.md)

    Links a recipient can check

    folder audit

  • What it is

    A 79-sign homophonic German letter cipher (two-digit numbers, doubled letters, 4000 = König), not the Orange-Saxony systems. Key aligned from 5109's cipher against the spaced passage Japikse prints as the Prince's cipher insertion (zero conflicts among C pairs). The cipher passage of 8246 is printed deciphered in Japikse, Correspondentie van Willem den Eerste I (1934), no.316, pp.343-344 (N0); our key independently regenerates that text.

    N0 (AUDIT.md): printed deciphered in Japikse 1934 no.316 pp.343-344; our re-decipherment pp.1-3 C 817, M 53, U 83 of 953 signs

    Links a recipient can check

    folder audit

  • What it is

    Three 1574 letters read under the same table as 4610-4616, so 4612's failure is not a general 1574 change. 5799 (3 Apr 1573) fails the table (0 of 146 numerals give French, matched control 81.5%): a different key. 5194 and 5797 not transcribed. 5811 and 4503 are printed in Groen van Prinsterer, Archives IV, Lettres CDLXXXIII (pp. 364-366) and CDLXXXIV (pp. 368-369, with omissions), cited in their own WVO records; our readings are re-decipherments that agree with those prints (N0).

    5811: C 616, I 81, M 792, U 53 of 1542; 4503: 237 tokens, I 7 M 219 U 11 (single settled reading); 5810 single pass (M 2771 of 3096); novelty: 5811 N0, 4503 N0 (AUDIT.md 'WV2 letters (V7)', 24 Sept 2026), 5810 not audited

    What the passage says, and why it matters

    confirms or adds detail -- Orange's printed reply carries the substance; fragments add Louis's proposals (convocation of Electors, French contacts)

    Letter. Louis of Nassau to his brother William of Orange. 3 June 1573, no place ("Pour Hollande", duplicate).
    KHA A 11/XIV D/13a, WVO 4610. No edition. Orange's reply of 17 June 1573 (Leiden) is printed: Groen van Prinsterer,
    *Archives* I/IV, Lettre CDXXVII, pp.156-160.

    Reading (`reading_4610.txt`; 1,545 tokens including clear words: C 1,098, M 101, I 58, U 288. Numerals only:
    1,222, C 1,073). Key aligned from the contemporary decipherments of 4613/4615. Words in braces are clear text or word
    signs; lower-case runs are decoded numerals with no word breaks. Readable fragments:
    > "Il fault que [vous?] pardonne de ce ... qui depesche ... ce lundy"; "de la rechute"; "semployer pour rapaiser";
    > "electeurs ... je luy ay propose une conuocation ... des electeurs"; "aduisez aux conditions ... proposees ... affin
    > [de] pouuoir ouurir ... la negotiation"; "leduc dalencon"; "trois cens cheuaux"; "capiteines frencois";
    > "strabourg"; "delapaix"

    *An apology for a delayed messenger. His relapse [of fever]. Efforts to appease. He proposed a convocation of the
    Electors and asks Orange's view of the conditions, so as to open a negotiation. The Duke of Alençon, 300 horse, French
    captains, Strasbourg, peace.* Most lines are fragmentary.

    Already known. Orange's reply answers it: "Vostre lettre du 3e jour de ce mois ... la fiebvre vous a de rechief
    quicté", the Emperor's answer to the princes about pacification, and three cavalry companies.

    What the cipher adds. Louis's side of it: the proposal to convene the Electors, and the French contacts (Alençon,
    French captains), which the reply only answers.

    Moment. *(context)* In June 1573 Haarlem was under siege (it fell on 12 July). Louis was in Germany seeking
    princely and French backing and a mediated peace. Orange held Holland and Zeeland alone.

    Significance: confirms or adds detail to what the edition gives. The printed reply carries the substance. The
    fragments add Louis's proposals.

    To
    resources@huygens.knaw.nl, cc info@huygens.knaw.nl
    Subject
    Cipher passages in six WVO letters (4610, 4611, 4616; 53, 57, 126) read with contemporary keys
    Body
    Dear editors of the Correspondence of William of Orange,

I run a small project that reads cipher passages in published letter images, with each reading checked by a separate search for any prior decipherment. Six letters in your William of Orange database carry cipher that the database records as unread. We have read them in part, with keys taken from contemporary decipherments of sibling letters in the same database, and located no prior decipherment in the principal editions after a logged search. None changes the story the editions tell; each adds a detail or shows the secret channel.

- WVO 4610, 4611, 4616, Lodewijk van Nassau to Orange, 3 June and 2 July 1573 and 12 April 1574 (two days before Mookerheyde). Key from the decipherments of 4613 and 4615. The cipher gives Louis's side of exchanges whose replies Groen prints (Archives, 1st ser., IV): a proposal to convene the Electors and French contacts; recruiting, with an offer of twenty companies for Friesland; and, in 1574, march and lodging.
- WVO 53, Orange to Elector August, 24 Oct 1561. Read without any key source (a cryptanalytic result, 238 of 364 signs at grade S): news of the Prince of Spain's marriage and the Netherlands, Vendôme and Navarre, and Orange's own judgement that it would come to nothing. The rumours are printed from other senders.
- WVO 57, August to Orange, 18 Nov 1561, read with the key of Orange's 1562 cipher: the Emperor's approach about electing Maximilian King of the Romans, told to Orange in secret within days of the Palatine letters Kluckhohn prints.
- WVO 126, Orange to August, 16 Sept 1564, read with a key from WVO 98: the Queen of Spain's miscarriage, printed from Saint-Sulpice; the cipher adds only the channel.

Readings, keys, per-token grades and the full search log for each: https://github.com/noautopilot/cipher-lab/tree/main/ciphers/lodewijk-van-nassau-1573-74 and https://github.com/noautopilot/cipher-lab/tree/main/ciphers/august-van-saksen-1561-64 (AUDIT.md in each). Images are your own record pages (brief?nr=4610, 4611, 4616, 53, 57, 126). If your editors know a decipherment of any of these, in the KHA or Dresden minutes or elsewhere, we would be glad to cite it.

If useful, we can hand over a table of the 73 WVO letters we found with cipher passages, marked which an edition prints in clear and which stand unread, and the keys as machine-readable tables, in whatever format suits the database. And if there are unread passages you would most like read, name them and we will try those first.

cipher-lab
https://github.com/NoAutopilot/cipher-lab

## Reply (received 26 Sept 2026, dr. Ineke Huysman, Huygens Instituut; pasted by the person; recorded as data)

Observations, each with what it changes here:

1. **WVO 53 and 126: the KHA holds twentieth-century copies in the Collectie Japikse.** Japikse printed cipher passages deciphered in his first volume (1934); these copies were preparation for a continuation that never appeared. She judges them the most likely place for an earlier decipherment, more so than the editions. -> Both audits list the Japikse copy as an unseen witness (AUDIT.md V3 and A2). An editor of the database now names it the first place to look, so the "no prior decipherment located" sentence for 53 and 126 must carry that witness by name until it is seen; the N-class question is referred to the verifier lane (ROOM, 26 Sept). Access needs a person at the KHA: ASKS row 67. 2. **126 and 57: the minutes (KHA A11-XIVi-04-26 "met een 'Zeitung'"; Dresden Loc. 9941/3 f.268-269 "met een 'Zettel'") may simply carry the text in clear.** -> Already in AUDIT.md (2.3, A2.2 item 2); keep the sentence in every summary of the results. 3. **The Palatine audience in Kluckhohn (nr. 148) dates from 30-31 December 1561, six weeks after August's letter of 18 November, not within days.** -> The audit had the date right (AUDIT.md D2.1 row 1, V3c row 9); the sent email and N4-READINGS.md said "within days" -- both corrected 26 Sept 2026. 4. **53 rests entirely on a recovered key, with a third of the signs uncertain: present it as a proposal, not a text.** -> Adopted: the status.json row, CONTRIBUTIONS.md and every summary say "proposed reading" for 53. 5. Two questions before she takes it further: (a) who is responsible for the project and how the work is divided between people and AI agents -- "it should be stated plainly in any contact with editors"; (b) what we intend to do with the results (publication, deposit with the holding archives, or only the repository). -> The person answers both; the disclosure sentence is now a standing rule for every outreach draft (outreach/README.md).

Dear Special Collections & Archives reference staff,

I direct a small open project on unsolved historical ciphers and send its letters myself; AI agents (Claude models) do the reading, the searches and the checking, and every step is logged in the public repository linked below. We are trying to read a letter John Armstrong, U.S. minister in Paris, sent to James Madison on 20 February 1808, in a numeral cipher that the State Department could not read; Madison thought it was a cipher "concerted with another correspondent".

James Bowdoin III was Armstrong's fellow commissioner in Paris in 1806-1807. The Massachusetts Historical Society's printed Bowdoin and Temple Papers (1907) give their exchanges of 1806-1807 and Bowdoin's cipher letters to George W. Erving, but not his letterbooks, which your department holds (the Bowdoin family collection lists letterbooks up to 1811).

Could you tell me whether the James Bowdoin III letterbook covering 1806-1811 contains any letter to or from Armstrong written partly in numbers, any copy of a cipher key, or any letter of January-May 1808 mentioning Armstrong's cipher? If there is anything of the kind, I would be grateful for a quote for scans of those pages only (a quote, please; I will confirm before ordering).

What we have tried so far, including the printed Bowdoin letters, is at https://github.com/NoAutopilot/cipher-lab/tree/main/ciphers/armstrong-madison-1808

Dear FDR Library research staff,

I direct a small open project on unsolved historical ciphers and send its letters myself; AI agents (Claude models) do the reading, the searches and the checking, and every step is logged in the public repository linked below. We are trying to read a letter John Armstrong, U.S. minister in Paris, sent to James Madison on 20 February 1808, written in a numeral cipher mixed with shorthand-like signs that the State Department could not read. Madison thought it was a cipher "concerted with another correspondent". In 1805 Armstrong is on record as keeping a private cipher with James Monroe.

Your Library's guide to the Hudson River Valley manuscript collections (Appendix I) describes the Aldrich Family Papers, 1770-1895, one microfilm roll of the Rokeby Collection: about 200 items, most on John Armstrong, over half on his French ministry, with correspondents including Madison, Monroe and Talleyrand; filmed in 1966 for the National Historical Publications Commission, with access restricted by permission of the collection's owners.

Could you tell me, first, whether the positive film at the Library may be consulted or copied by a researcher at a distance, and whose permission that needs; and second, whether the roll has an item list showing any cipher key, numbered code table, or letter or draft of 1805-1808 written partly in numbers? If copies are possible, I would be grateful for a quote for the relevant frames (a quote only, please; I will confirm before ordering).

A key or a second letter in the same cipher is the one thing that could make the 1808 letter readable. What we have tried so far is at https://github.com/NoAutopilot/cipher-lab/tree/main/ciphers/armstrong-madison-1808

Dear Klingenstein Library reference staff,

I direct a small open project on unsolved historical ciphers and send its letters myself; AI agents (Claude models) do the reading, the searches and the checking, and every step is logged in the public repository linked below. We are trying to read a letter John Armstrong (1758-1843), U.S. minister in Paris, sent to James Madison on 20 February 1808. It is in a numeral cipher mixed with shorthand-like signs that the State Department could not read; Madison thought it was a cipher "concerted with another correspondent". In 1805 Armstrong is on record as keeping a private cipher with James Monroe, so he did keep ciphers of his own outside the Department's.

Your library holds the largest body of Armstrong's own papers that we know of (about 200 letters 1777-1843, a microfilm of privately owned letters 1798-1833, a letterbook of 1804 and photostats of other collections, as the U.S. House's history office lists them). No finding aid for them was reachable from where we work.

Could you tell me whether the Armstrong papers contain any cipher key or numbered code table, any letter or draft of 1805-1808 written partly in numbers, a retained copy or draft of the 20 February 1808 letter, or a letterbook later than 1804? If something of the kind is there, I would be grateful for a quote for scans of those items only (a quote, please; I will confirm before ordering).

A key or a second letter in the same cipher is the one thing that could make the 1808 letter readable. What we have tried so far is at https://github.com/NoAutopilot/cipher-lab/tree/main/ciphers/armstrong-madison-1808

Dear Manuscripts and Archives Division reference staff,

I direct a small open project on unsolved historical ciphers and send its letters myself; AI agents (Claude models) do the reading, the searches of the printed editions and the checking, and every step is logged in the public repository linked below. We are working on a letter John Armstrong, U.S. minister in Paris, sent to James Madison on 20 February 1808, written in a numeral cipher that the State Department could not read. Madison wrote to Jefferson on 15 May 1808 that "the undecyphered letter from A. was probably misaddressed" and was in a cipher "concerted with another correspondent"; an editor of the Papers of James Madison reads that remark as referring to the 20 February letter.

We found a precedent in the printed Madison Papers. On 2 April 1805 Armstrong told Madison that a copy of his letter of 18 March 1805 to James Monroe, then at Madrid, had gone to Washington in "the cypher established between him [Monroe] and me" instead of the Department's code (Founders Online, Madison Papers, Secretary of State Series 9, Armstrong to Madison 18 March and 2 April 1805). Monroe's incoming letters from his Madrid mission are partly at the New York Public Library (the editors cite, for example, Erving to Monroe, 2 March 1805, to the NYPL James Monroe papers), and the Library of Congress's own Monroe finding aid places Monroe's letterbook for November 1804 to May 1805 at the NYPL. We could not establish where the originals of Armstrong's March-April 1805 letters to Monroe are; the Library of Congress Monroe papers we checked do not seem to have them.

I would like to ask two things about the James Monroe papers (MssCol 2035). First, whether the collection holds Armstrong's letters to Monroe of 12 and 18 March and 1 and 5 April 1805, any other Armstrong letter to Monroe partly in cipher (1804-1808), and anything that looks like a cipher key or table shared by the two men, together with Monroe's letterbook for November 1804 to May 1805. Second, if they are there, what it would cost to have scans made of those items (a quote only, please; I will confirm before any order).

A key, or a second letter in the same cipher, is the one thing that could make the 1808 letter readable. Everything we have done so far, including what we ruled out, is at https://github.com/NoAutopilot/cipher-lab/tree/main/ciphers/armstrong-madison-1808

[firma]

Hello, and thank you for the project. We checked your unsolved-ciphers repository and decode-catalog.csv (24-25 Sept 2026) for this shelfmark, ark and sender/recipient and found no entry, so this is a candidate addition.

BnF Espagnol 144 f.22r-22v (within the Espagnol 142-144 codex, TOME III; the BnF finding aid's item 11), an unsigned Spanish instruction to the baron (abbé) de Mercy, dated Barneton 6 June 1648, has been read in part by our own cryptanalysis, in the wording of our audit: "BnF Espagnol 144 f.22, an unsigned Spanish instruction to the abbé (Baron) de Mercy dated Barneton 6 June 1648, has been read in part by our own cryptanalysis (496 of 522 code tokens at grade S, 26 at M, none from a key or known plaintext). No prior decipherment of this leaf and no print of its plaintext were located after three logged searches (25-28 Sept 2026, this AUDIT.md) that now cover the principal editions, catalogues and project pages; internal or unpublished work is not excluded. The substance of the mission it sets out is in print: Lonchay, Cuvelier and Lefèvre, Correspondance de la Cour d'Espagne IV (1933) no. 183, p. 71, calendars Leopold Wilhelm's own dispatch to Philip IV of June 1648 (Brussels, Secrétairerie d'État et de Guerre, reg. 240, fol. 201) as reporting that someone had been asked to ask the Elector of Brandenburg whether 3,000 infantry could be raised in his states for a corps to operate in Flanders. The reading is a cryptanalytic result, not confirmed by a key or a clear copy."

Prior print this rests on: Lonchay, Cuvelier and Lefèvre, *Correspondance de la Cour d'Espagne* IV (1933) no. 183, p. 71 (above; the mission's substance, not the instruction's own text). A further, closer-dated but unprinted lead: Henri Lonchay, *La rivalité de la France et de l'Espagne aux Pays-Bas (1635-1700)* (1896), p.445 n.2, cites an instruction to Mercy of 15 April 1648 (Brussels S.E.E. t. LXIV f.16) without printing it, cross-referencing ("Cf.") a further Leopold Wilhelm dispatch to the king of 30 August 1648 (t. LXV f.181) whose content it does not give:
https://archive.org/details/la-rivalite-de-la-france-et-d-espagne-aux-pays-bas-1635-1700/page/445

- Images: https://gallica.bnf.fr/ark:/12148/btv1b10035717h/f58.item (f.22r) and /f59.item (f.22v)
- BnF finding aid: https://archivesetmanuscrits.bnf.fr/ark:/12148/cc347546
- Transcription, key, reading (regenerated by script), per-token grades and search log:
https://github.com/noautopilot/cipher-lab/tree/main/ciphers/espagnol142-mercy-1648
and https://github.com/noautopilot/cipher-lab/blob/main/ciphers/espagnol142-mercy-1648/AUDIT.md

Corrections welcome, especially on the M-graded signs, and on whether a period key for this office is already known to you (we have not found one; DECODE's Brussels Secrétairerie d'État et de Guerre "chiffres 1647-98" series, records 958-965, was checked 27 Sept 2026 and ruled out by design -- each is a mixed alphabet/graphic-sign/numerical nomenclature key of more than 20 entries, not a match for this target's fixed-length, 38-value, letter-only code).

Madame,

Je me permets de vous écrire sur la suggestion du Département des Manuscrits de la BnF, à qui j'avais écrit le 1er octobre 2026 au sujet d'un feuillet chiffré d'Espagnol 144.

Je dirige un petit projet personnel, non commercial et sans financement (« Cipher Lab »), consacré aux lettres chiffrées anciennes ; c'est moi qui dirige le projet et qui écris, et le travail lui-même (transcriptions, recherches dans les éditions, vérifications) est fait par des agents d'intelligence artificielle (modèles Claude), dans un dépôt ouvert où chaque étape est journalisée. Nous sommes des amateurs passionnés, et vous êtes une spécialiste de l'histoire du chiffre : je vous écris simplement pour vous demander si, et comment, nous pourrions vous être utiles.

Pour vous donner une idée de ce que nous faisons : nous avons transcrit signe par signe plusieurs lettres chiffrées de la BnF (Nevers, Birago, Gramont, Danzay, entre autres), avec les images, les tables de signes et les essais de clés, tout en accès libre. Pour Espagnol 144, f. 22 (instruction en espagnol, non signée, à l'abbé de Mercy, Barneton, 6 juin 1648), nous proposons une lecture partielle par cryptanalyse, sans clé d'époque, avec huit corrections de transcription et deux redécoupages de D. Bourdeau (1er octobre 2026), vérifiés par nous sur l'image : sur 529 groupes chiffrés, 41 restent incertains (environ 8 %), et rien n'est confirmé par une clé ou un texte connu ; ce n'est qu'une lecture proposée. La substance de la mission est déjà imprimée (Lonchay, Cuvelier et Lefèvre, *Correspondance de la Cour d'Espagne* IV, 1933, no 183, p. 71), et une copie en clair pourrait se trouver dans les registres de la Secrétairerie d'État et de Guerre à Bruxelles, que nous n'avons pas vus.

Si une partie de ce travail peut vous servir, ou s'il y a dans vos recherches une tâche que nous pourrions prendre en charge (transcrire des pièces chiffrées, trier des signes, confronter des clés connues à des lettres, préparer des données), nous le ferions très volontiers, à votre manière et selon vos conventions. Et si ce n'est pas le moment, je comprendrai tout à fait.

Notre dossier sur ce feuillet : https://github.com/noautopilot/cipher-lab/tree/main/ciphers/espagnol142-mercy-1648 ; le feuillet sur Gallica : https://gallica.bnf.fr/ark:/12148/btv1b10035717h/f58.item (22r) et https://gallica.bnf.fr/ark:/12148/btv1b10035717h/f59.item (22v)

Avec mes remerciements,

[SIGN-OFF]

---

Dear Dr Desenclos,

I am writing at the suggestion of the BnF Département des Manuscrits, to whom I wrote on 1 October 2026 about a cipher leaf in Espagnol 144.

I direct a small personal project, non-commercial and unfunded ("Cipher Lab"), on early cipher letters; I direct it and write, and the work itself (transcriptions, searches of the editions, checks) is done by AI agents (Claude models) in an open repository where every step is logged. We are passionate amateurs, and you are an expert in the history of ciphers: I am writing simply to ask whether, and how, we could be of use to you.

To give you an idea of what we do: we have transcribed several BnF cipher letters sign by sign (Nevers, Birago, Gramont, Danzay, among others), with the images, sign tables and key tests, all openly available. For Espagnol 144 f. 22 (an unsigned Spanish instruction to the abbé de Mercy, Barneton, 6 June 1648) we propose a partial reading by cryptanalysis, without a period key, with eight transcription corrections and two re-cuts by D. Bourdeau (1 October 2026) checked by us on the image: of 529 code groups, 41 remain uncertain (about 8%), and nothing is confirmed by a key or a known text; it is only a proposed reading. The substance of the mission is already in print (Lonchay, Cuvelier and Lefèvre, *Correspondance de la Cour d'Espagne* IV, 1933, no. 183, p. 71), and a clear copy may survive in the registers of the Secrétairerie d'État et de Guerre in Brussels, which we have not seen.

If any of this work could be useful to you, or if there is a task in your research we could take on (transcribing cipher pieces, sorting signs, testing known keys against letters, preparing data), we would be very glad to do it, in your way and to your conventions. And if now is not the time, I will entirely understand.

Our folder on this leaf: https://github.com/noautopilot/cipher-lab/tree/main/ciphers/espagnol142-mercy-1648; the leaf on Gallica: https://gallica.bnf.fr/ark:/12148/btv1b10035717h/f58.item (22r) and https://gallica.bnf.fr/ark:/12148/btv1b10035717h/f59.item (22v)

With thanks,

[SIGN-OFF]

Pott's 1896 decipherment of a cipher letter to Anthony Bacon (Lambeth MS 649) -- does her manuscript survive? target: ciphers/lambeth-bacon-649 (ASKS row: see ASKS.md, A2P4-LAMB3) sign-off: [SIGN-OFF]

Dear Francis Bacon Society,

I direct a small open research project on unread historical cipher letters and send every message myself; the searches of the printed sources and the catalogue checks behind this enquiry were done by AI agents (Claude models) working in an open repository, every step logged at https://github.com/noautopilot/cipher-lab/tree/main/ciphers/lambeth-bacon-649 .

I am writing to ask whether a manuscript once in the Society's possession still survives. In Baconiana no. 74 (June 1928, p. 284), the editor answers a correspondent: "The letter from 'T. Moresin' in French to Anthony Bacon containing cypher ... Reference -- No. 649. Tenison MSS. Lambeth Palace Library. Mrs. C. M. Pott deciphered and translated this letter in 1896, and her MS. is in our possession" (https://archive.org/details/baconiana_1928-06_19_74).

We also found what appears to be Mrs Pott's own printed account of that work: "Numbers Ten and Eleven", signed C. M. P., Baconiana vol. V (new series) no. 17, January 1897, pp. 23-29 (https://archive.org/details/1897-vol-v-new-series-no-17-20/page/23/mode/1up). It describes "a remarkable French letter written in cipher (figures)" among the Lambeth MSS., gives some of the cipher values and a few phrases of the French, and says she hoped "to reproduce this curious and enigmatical letter at full length". We have not found that full reproduction in Baconiana 1897-1898 or in the issues of the Society's journal from 1886 to 1895 that we could find on the Internet Archive (no full 1891 issue was found there), which we searched in full text.

Lambeth Palace Library's catalogue lists Thomas Moresin's French letters to Anthony Bacon at MS 649, ff. 328, 485-489 and 496-508, and an item between them, ff. 490-495, catalogued only as "Letters in cypher to BACON (Anthony)". It is this item we are trying to read. Neither Mrs Pott's article nor the 1928 note names a folio, so we do not know whether her letter is one of ff. 490-495.

I would be grateful to know: (1) whether Mrs Pott's 1896 manuscript decipherment and translation is still in the Society's collection or archive; (2) if so, whether it names the folio of the letter, and (3) whether a scan or photograph of it could be shared for research, on whatever terms the Society sets. Any credit you wish would of course be given in the repository.

With thanks,

[SIGN-OFF]

If the parent prefers Dutch, the Dutch text goes above a separator with this English text beneath it.

Dear Sir or Madam,

I direct a small research project, cipher-lab, that studies unsolved historical ciphers; I write and send every message myself, while the reading of documents, the searches of editions and catalogues, and the checking of results are done by AI agents (Claude models) working in an open repository where every step is logged (https://github.com/NoAutopilot/cipher-lab). I am writing to ask whether a digital scan of one letter in the Koninklijk Huisarchief can be supplied, and at what fee.

The item is inventory A11, A11-XIVb-15-43: "Van Willem van Hessen", 28 January 1567 (Wilhelm of Hesse, later Landgrave Wilhelm IV of Hesse-Kassel, to William of Orange, from Kassel), https://www.koninklijkeverzamelingen.nl/archief/a/a11/a11-xiv/a11-xivb/a11-xivb-15-43/. Your record shows no scans for it, and the Huygens Instituut's database of William of Orange's correspondence (WVO letter 1127, https://resources.huygens.knaw.nl/wvo/app/brief?nr=1127) lists no image of the original either.

The reason for the request: the WVO record notes that part of this original is in a cipher that has not been solved ("Een gedeelte van het origineel is in onopgelost cijferschrift"), and that the sender's draft in the Hessisches Staatsarchiv Marburg (Bestand 3II, Korr. 1567, f. 151r-152v) gives the complete text. The draft is online (https://resources.huygens.knaw.nl/media/wvo/images/01000-01999/01127.pdf). We would align the enciphered passage of the original with the corresponding passage of the draft, which should recover the cipher's key, or show where the fair copy departs from the draft. We have not found the letter printed: it is not in Groen van Prinsterer's Archives ou correspondance inédite de la Maison d'Orange-Nassau, 1e série, t. III (1836), whose 1567 section we searched (table of contents pp. xci-xcvi and the full text) (https://archive.org/details/archivesoucorre04housgoog).

My request is:

1. A digital scan or photograph, at a resolution where the cipher signs can be read, of every page of A11-XIVb-15-43, including the enciphered passage, and of the undated, unsigned loose note that the WVO record describes as enclosed with it, if it is kept with the item. 2. The fee for this, and how to pay it. 3. If remote reproduction is not possible, the procedure for a reading-room visit, and whether the toestemmingsformulier on your website is the right first step for this.

The purpose is scholarly research only, with no commercial use; any result would cite the Koninklijk Huisarchief and the item's inventory number, in whatever form you prescribe, and the request and its outcome are recorded in the repository folder for this letter (https://github.com/NoAutopilot/cipher-lab/tree/main/ciphers/willem-van-hessen-1567).

Thank you for considering this.

With kind regards,

[SIGN-OFF]

Dear Registrar,

I direct a small open research project on unread historical ciphers and I send every message myself; the searching, transcription and comparison behind this enquiry were done by AI agents (Claude models) working in an open repository where every step is logged: https://github.com/noautopilot/cipher-lab/tree/main/ciphers/moray-wood-1568

We are working on a short cipher postscript in a letter from the Regent Moray to John Wood, 13 July 1568 (British Library, Add MS 32091 ff.213-214). We have a partial proposed reading of it, with about an eighth of the signs still uncertain, and what would settle it is any other text written in the same cipher, or a key sheet for it.

The Historical Manuscripts Commission's Sixth Report (p.638), describing the Earl of Moray's papers then at Donibristle, reports cipher papers among them that, as far as we have found, have never been reproduced. We understand these papers are now registered with you as NRAS217, and another researcher's notes place them in Box 45; we have not been able to confirm that ourselves.

I would be grateful to know: (1) whether the NRAS217 survey lists cipher letters, cipher papers or a key of the 1560s, and under which box and item numbers; (2) whether Moray's letters to John Wood of August-September 1568 are listed there; and (3) how a request to see or obtain copies of such items should be made, given that the collection is privately held.

A single page from the survey listing, if it can be shared, would be enough for us to tell whether a copy request is worth making.

Thank you for your help.

[SIGN-OFF]

# TNA copy order: John Wroth to Cecil, 1596-98, with his cipher key (SP 81/7/239 + two SP 81/8 letters)

## What to order (three records, page check first)

| # | Reference | Discovery record | Date | Catalogue description (TNA) | Why | |---|---|---|---|---|---| | 1 | SP 81/7/239 | https://discovery.nationalarchives.gov.uk/details/r/C7771054 | 1596 Sept. 7 | "Folio 239: Jn. Wrothe to Cecil with cipher key." | the key sheet Wroth sent Cecil from Cassel | | 2 | SP 81/8/29 | https://discovery.nationalarchives.gov.uk/details/r/C7771077 | 1598 Jan. 12 | "Folio 29: Jn. Wrothe to Cecil." | a later Wroth letter the key could read, if it carries cipher | | 3 | SP 81/8/31 | https://discovery.nationalarchives.gov.uk/details/r/C7771078 | 1598 Jan. 17 | "Folio 31: Jn. Wrothe to Cecil." | the same |

All three are `digitised: false` in the Discovery API (details records read 3 Oct 2026). These are the only Wroth letters the Discovery API returns in SP 81 for "Wroth"/"Wrothe" (four items in all); the fourth, SP 81/7/234 (8 Aug 1596), was written before the key was sent and is left out. Neither SP 81/8 description mentions cipher, so items 2 and 3 are the speculative part of the order: TNA's page says a page check is non-refundable once work starts and may be unsuccessful if the enquiry is speculative. If only one item is ordered, order item 1.

Printed calendar line for item 1 (R. B. Wernham, *List and Analysis of State Papers, Foreign Series, Elizabeth I*, vol. VII, 1596, PRO 2000, p. 29, read as Google Books snippets of volume hG4pAAAAYAAJ, not a page image): "GS15 Sept 7 John Wrothe to Cecil. Cassel fo. 226 Holograph. Enclosing key to a cipher. p. See Analysis 331." (fo. 226 appears to be the older foliation of what TNA now numbers 239: same writer, date and description; inferred, not checked against the volume.)

## Price (TNA's own fee page)

https://www.nationalarchives.gov.uk/help-with-your-research/record-copying/fees/ , read 3 Oct 2026 17:19 UTC: Page Check £9.92 per record; Digital copy £1.52 per copy (the larger-format digital copy is £11.95). The record- copying page says the process takes approximately 29 working days from start to finish.

- Page checks: 3 x £9.92 = £29.76
- Digital copies, estimated 2 images per item (recto and verso; the real count comes back with the page check): 6 x £1.52 = £9.12
- **About £38.88 in all**, item 1 alone about £12.96.

These three could also ride the consolidated TNA batch in outreach/tna-page-copy-batch.md (ASKS row 73) as extra records, since each record is basketed separately anyway.

## Steps for the person

1. Open each Discovery link above; at the bottom of the record click "Order a copy" and follow the form (page check, digital copy, research quality, standard size). 2. If the form has a free-text box for what is wanted, paste the note below. 3. Record the order date (no payment details) in ASKS row 121 and in CONTRIBUTIONS.md's row for this slug.

## Note for the free-text box (if the form has one)

I direct a small research project on historical cipher letters and place this order myself; the catalogue searches and the reading of the printed calendars behind it were done by AI agents (Claude models) working in an open repository, with every step logged. I would like a digital copy of the whole folio, both sides and any enclosure or slip, including any page carrying cipher symbols, numbers or a key table. For SP 81/7/239 we have the catalogue line "with cipher key" and Wernham's List and Analysis (1596, GS15: "Holograph. Enclosing key to a cipher"). For SP 81/8/29 and /31 the whole letter is wanted, whether or not any part is in cipher.

Hall of fame

Public citations of this project's work by someone outside the repository: a credit, a link, a correction adopted, co-authorship or a reply that became public. CITATIONS.md is the record; the owner is never named (rule 9), the repository is.

3 public citations since 23 Sept 2026

  • 24 Sept 2026S. Tomokiyo, Cryptiana

    unsolved.htm, "Ciphers related to Sir Francis Walsingham" → ciphers/bowes-walsingham-1583

    "notified me of his solution on 24 September 2026. He found Robert Bowes' two letters ... are in The Correspondence of Robert Bowes (1842) ... and CSP Scotland, vi (1910)."

    credit + link to the repository folder; adopted three manuscript corrections (MAGNYVIL for "Manningvile", the "and" handwriting abbreviation, Montrosse = code 189) that our reading, from the transcription alone, did not have

  • 26 Sept 2026S. Tomokiyo, Cryptiana

    A Cipher between Louise of Savoy and Nicolas Raince (1525), Cryptiana blog, Sept 2026 → ciphers/dupuy452-carpi-1520 (the Raince 25 Oct 1525 correction, N0), fr2980-gramont AUDIT.md, fr20140-danzay-1557 AUDIT.md

    "I learned from [the repository's author] that a letter to her (25 October 1525) from Nicolas Raince ... in Dupuy 452 used Nicolas Raince's cipher (1526) I reconstructed"; "[he] also published reading of some letters mentioned in my articles by using the respective keys mentioned"

    a whole post on the Raince letter our 24 Sept note pointed him to (Dupuy 452 f.28, his 1526 key, Jacqueton 1892), with the sibling Raince-Robertet letter f.24 he adds himself; credits the repository's author by name and links our Gramont and Danzay AUDIT.md files as published readings made with his keys

  • 1 Oct 2026D. Bourdeau, Unsolved Historical Ciphers (dbourdeau/cyphersolver)

    Mercy 1648, a write-up page posted 1 Oct 2026, files in `targets/mercy1648/` (our originals kept unchanged in `cl/`); reply on issue 16 → ciphers/espagnol142-mercy-1648 (transcription, ciphertext-only key, reading of the cipher runs)

    "NoAutopilot (cipher-lab) recovered the key by annealing on Spanish n-grams, with no plaintext."; "The transcription, the key and the reading of the cipher runs are NoAutopilot's ...; the corrections and the identifications on this page are ours."

    made it a target in his repository, credits the transcription, the key and the reading to the repository and links the folder and issue 16; checked f.22r-v on the full-resolution Gallica images and corrected eight tokens (four 'M' codes above 34 are two digits written together: 65, 52, 48 x2, 72; two glued 26 = `2 6`; one 19 = an open 14), re-cut two tokens in r10 by sense (M), identified the box sign as the comte de Saint-Ibal (from the clear f.21r, 13 Apr 1648, and f.20r, 8 Feb) and 'copurad lon burgsto rf' as Konrad von Burgsdorff, Brandenburg's Oberkammerherr; counts 521 of 529 tokens read (98.5%), v04's code 15 still open, and asks for our view of it
